# Round-two candidates: record then adversarial verdicts (refuted OVERRIDES record)


## Node-RED

```json
{
 "name": "Node-RED",
 "repo_url": "https://github.com/node-red/node-red",
 "license_spdx": "Apache-2.0",
 "license_is_osi_approved": true,
 "license_restrictions": "None that affect us. The LICENSE file at the repo root is the verbatim Apache License 2.0 (\"Apache License Version 2.0, January 2004\"), and GitHub's repo metadata agrees (`license.spdx_id == \"Apache-2.0\"`). The only restricting clauses are the standard Apache ones: patent-retaliation termination (\"If You institute patent litigation against any entity ... any patent licenses granted to You under this License for that Work shall terminate as of the date such litigation is filed\"), the trademark carve-out (\"This License does not grant permission to use the trade names, trademarks, service marks, or product names of the Licensor\"), attribution/NOTICE retention on redistribution, and the AS-IS warranty and liability disclaimers. Self-hosted commercial internal use is unambiguously permitted and requires no notice, since we are not redistributing. Every source file I read carries the matching Apache-2.0 header (e.g. `packages/node_modules/node-red/red.js`, `packages/node_modules/@node-red/nodes/core/function/10-function.js`). No file disagreed. The streaming node we would actually depend on, `@inductiv/node-red-openai-api`, is separately MIT (\"Permission is hereby granted, free of charge, to any person obtaining a copy of this software ... without restriction\"), copyright Allan Bunch 2023 \u2014 also fine.",
 "enterprise_gated_paths": "None. I grepped the entire runtime, editor-api and node-red packages case-insensitively for `enterprise`, `license key`, `premium` and `paid tier`, excluding Apache boilerplate and package.json `\"license\"` fields, and got zero hits. There is no open-core split, no dual-licensed directory, and nothing resembling round one's failure shape of an enterprise middleware imported unconditionally at boot \u2014 `packages/node_modules/node-red/lib/red.js` requires only the runtime and editor-api, both Apache-2.0 in-repo. FlowFuse is a commercial product built around Node-RED by a separate company; it is not in this repo and no capability we need lives there. Two features we care about are gated behind a *settings flag* rather than a paid tier, which is worth calling out because they are off by default: Projects (git-backed flows) has `projects: { enabled: false }` in the default `settings.js` and is documented as \"in preview mode ... it must be enabled in the settings file\" or via `NODE_RED_ENABLE_PROJECTS=true`; and `functionExternalModules` ships `true`, which is a security posture question, not a licensing one.",
 "self_host": "Fully self-hostable with no hosted control plane of any kind. There is no telemetry callout, no license check, no registry ping at boot. Official container images come from the project's own `node-red/node-red-docker` repo (540 stars, last pushed 2026-09-09, same day as the 4.1.15 release), published as `nodered/node-red` \u2014 so a local docker-compose service is the intended deployment, one image plus a volume for `/data`. Alternatively, and more interesting for our seam, it runs entirely in-process inside our existing Node service with no separate container at all (see embeddable_in_node). Node >= 22.9 is enforced at startup in `red.js` via a semver check that hard-exits below that. Everything we need \u2014 the editor, the Function node, git-backed Projects, the admin HTTP API for scripted deploys \u2014 is in the Apache-2.0 tree.",
 "streams_token_level": true,
 "streaming_transport": "There are two separable questions, and the answer differs. (1) Out of a *node* into the flow: the mechanism is `node.send()` called repeatedly, once per model chunk. Verified in `src/chat/methods.js` of `@inductiv/node-red-openai-api`: when `stream: true`, `for await (const chunk of response) { if (typeof chunk === \"object\") { const newMsg = { ...parameters.msg, payload: chunk }; _node.send(newMsg); } }` \u2014 and the streaming branch returns `undefined`, so the generic handler in `node.js` (`.then((payload) => { if (payload !== undefined) { msg.payload = payload; node.send(msg); } })`) does not also emit a buffered copy. Identical loops exist in `src/responses/methods.js` (`streamResponse`), `src/runs/methods.js` (three of them) and `src/images/methods.js`. Chunk shape is the raw OpenAI SDK object on `msg.payload` \u2014 a `ChatCompletionChunk` or `ResponseStreamEvent`, so text is at `msg.payload.choices[0].delta.content` or the `response.output_text.delta` event. (2) Out of the *runtime* to a host: the honest answer for our architecture is in-process, not over a socket. Because we embed (`require(\"node-red\")` in the same process), the host registers a callback and gets each chunk synchronously \u2014 either via `functionGlobalContext` (host puts an EventEmitter or a queue in `RED.init` settings; a Function node calls `global.get('relay').push(chunk)`), or via the exported `RED.hooks` engine (`lib/red.js` exports `hooks: runtime.hooks`, and `@node-red/util/lib/hooks.js` defines `onSend`, `preRoute`, `postDeliver`, `onReceive`, triggered per SendEvent in `runtime/lib/flows/Flow.js`). Either way each chunk crosses into our code as it is produced, and we feed the voice channel's async-iterable method directly. Over the network, the only core node that emits incrementally is `websocket out`: its input handler calls `node.serverConfig.reply(...)` or `broadcast(payload)` once per message, so N messages become N WS frames. Core `http response` cannot stream at all \u2014 see streaming_caveats.",
 "streaming_evidence": "Source, not docs. Per-chunk emission: `src/chat/methods.js` and `src/responses/methods.js` in https://github.com/allanbunch/node-red-openai-api (the `for await` / `_node.send(newMsg)` loops quoted above), plus `node.js` showing the non-streaming path is the one that returns a single payload. The underlying primitive is core and documented: https://nodered.org/docs/user-guide/writing-functions states that if a function must do async work \"it cannot return the message at the end of the function\" and must instead \"make use of the `node.send()` function\", with the example `doSomeAsyncWork(msg, function(result) { msg.payload = result; node.send(msg); }); return;` \u2014 so sends after return, and repeated sends, are the supported model. Host-side receipt: `packages/node_modules/node-red/lib/red.js` lines 154-181 export `util`, `events` and `hooks`; `packages/node_modules/@node-red/util/lib/hooks.js` lines 5-9 and 44-48 define the hook names and state that `onSend` is \"passed an array of `SendEvent` objects. The messages inside these objects are exactly what the node has passed to `node.send`\"; `packages/node_modules/@node-red/runtime/lib/flows/Flow.js` lines 790-846 trigger them per send. WS frame-per-message: `packages/node_modules/@node-red/nodes/core/network/22-websocket.js` lines 429-457. Buffered HTTP: `packages/node_modules/@node-red/nodes/core/network/21-httpin.js` `HTTPOut`, lines 380-448.",
 "streaming_caveats": "Four, and the first two would bite us. (a) **Core Node-RED has zero LLM nodes.** I listed every core node directory (`common`, `function`, `network`, `parsers`, `sequence`, `storage`) and grepped case-insensitively for `openai|anthropic|llm|completion` \u2014 the only hits were incidental (`10-mqtt.js`, `10-function.js`). So the entire streaming capability rests on `@inductiv/node-red-openai-api`, a third-party package with a single maintainer (Allan Bunch), version 6.40.0 tracking the official `openai` SDK. That is a real key-person dependency on the one component that satisfies our hardest requirement. (b) **The core `http response` node cannot stream, at all.** `HTTPOut` computes `Buffer.byteLength(msg.payload)`, calls `msg.res._res.set('content-length', len)` and then `msg.res._res.status(statusCode).send(msg.payload)` \u2014 one complete body, no `res.write`, no `flushHeaders`, no `text/event-stream`, no chunked path anywhere in the file. So there is no SSE endpoint. Relatedly the core `http request` node sets `opts.responseType = 'buffer'`, meaning you cannot even proxy an upstream SSE stream through it \u2014 which is exactly why the streaming path must go through the contrib node's embedded SDK. (c) **No agent loop exists.** Nothing in Node-RED or in the OpenAI node runs a tool-call iteration; the node is a thin dynamic dispatcher onto SDK methods. A turn with tool calls is a loop *we wire by hand* on the canvas: stream chunks out, detect a `tool_calls` delta, fan to a Function node, feed the result back into a second OpenAI node call. Streaming survives this \u2014 each invocation streams independently, so text before and after a tool call both arrive incrementally \u2014 but the orchestration is our design, not a feature we inherit. (d) **A standard SSE or OpenAI client cannot parse the frames.** Over `websocket out`, `payload = RED.util.ensureString(msg.payload)` (or `JSON.stringify(msg)` in wholemsg mode) per frame \u2014 raw values with no `data:` prefix, no `\\n\\n` terminator, no `[DONE]` sentinel. In-process the \"frame\" is a JS object. Either way we write a small adapter; trivial given we are already in the same process, but do not expect drop-in client compatibility.",
 "tools_in_builder": "Yes, and this is Node-RED's strongest card \u2014 genuine implementation authoring, not definition-only forwarding. The Function node accepts arbitrary JavaScript typed into the browser and stored as a node property in `flows.json`; the runtime compiles it with `new vm.Script(functionText, createVMOpt(node, \"\"))` and runs it via `script.runInContext(context, opts)` (`core/function/10-function.js` lines 426-504). The executor is therefore Node's own `vm` module inside the Node-RED process \u2014 same event loop, same heap. The sandbox object handed to `vm.createContext` (lines 233-370) provides `console`, `util`, `Buffer`, `URL`, `URLSearchParams`, `Date`, a filtered `RED.util`, `context`/`flow`/`global` stores, `env.get`, wrapped `setTimeout`/`setInterval`, and a `node` fa\u00e7ade with `send`, `status`, `error`, `log` and `linkcall`. Two escalations matter for tools: the editor UI can attach npm modules per Function node (`node.libs`, loaded by `RED.import(module.module)` into a chosen sandbox variable) with `functionExternalModules: true` in the shipped default settings \u2014 so a browser-authored tool can `require` real libraries; and the core `exec` node runs arbitrary shell (`child_process.spawn`/`exec`, `90-exec.js` lines 19-158) configured entirely from the UI. Note `fetch` is *not* in the sandbox, so an HTTP-calling tool needs either an external module or a wired `http request` node. Also `node.on(\"input\", ...)` is explicitly blocked inside a Function node (\"function.error.inputListener\"). Net: a non-engineer-adjacent author can write a complete tool implementation in the builder, and `node.send()` inside it is the same primitive that carries streamed chunks.",
 "sandbox_risk": "High, by design, and it must be treated as such. Node's `vm` module is not a security boundary \u2014 it provides no isolation against a determined author, and Node-RED does not pretend otherwise: it hands the Function node a context deliberately narrower than global (no `process`, no `require`, no `fetch`) but layers two documented, UI-reachable arbitrary-code paths on top. The `exec` node spawns arbitrary shell commands as the Node-RED process user via `child_process.spawn` and `child_process.exec` (`core/function/90-exec.js`), and `functionExternalModules` defaults to `true` in the shipped `settings.js` (line 526), letting an editor user pull any npm module into the Function sandbox through `RED.import`. Compounding this, https://nodered.org/docs/user-guide/runtime/securing-node-red states plainly: \"By default, the Node-RED editor is not secured - anyone who can access its IP address can access the editor and deploy changes.\" The correct model is therefore **editor access equals code execution in our Node process, at our service's privilege** \u2014 so if we embed, the editor must sit behind auth on a non-public path, never on the same unauthenticated surface as the ConversationRelay endpoints. On CVEs, I will not overclaim: GitHub's advisory database lists exactly two for node-red/node-red, **CVE-2021-21298** (GHSA-m33v-338h-4v9f, high, path traversal via the Projects API) and **CVE-2021-21297** (GHSA-xp9c-82x8-7f67, medium, prototype pollution via mis-use of the admin API), both patched in 1.2.8 and both long superseded by the 5.x line. Neither is a Function-node sandbox escape, and I found no advisory that is \u2014 the risk here is designed behaviour plus an unauthenticated-by-default editor, not an unpatched bug.",
 "prompt_versioning": "Yes, and unusually well \u2014 prompt and tool selection version *atomically*, because they are the same artifact. Flows are a single JSON document (`flows.json`) containing every node's properties, so the system prompt text (in a Template or Change node, or the payload the OpenAI node reads) and the tool wiring (which Function nodes exist and how they are connected) sit in one file and move in one commit. The Projects feature makes that git-native inside the editor: `runtime/lib/storage/localfilesystem/projects/` with a real `git/` subdirectory driving `git` via `runGitCommand` (`child_process.spawn`, no shell, with an argv guard), and https://nodered.org/docs/user-guide/projects/ confirms projects are \"backed by a Git repository, meaning all of the files are fully version controlled\", with a history tab giving \"a list of all commits in the repository\" and per-file diffs. Rollback is git-shaped but partial: the docs say the editor \"does not expose all of the various options git provides\", specifically not rebase or force-push, \"You can still do those things by falling back to the command line.\" Activation is a Deploy from the editor \u2014 no process restart and no redeploy of our service \u2014 and it is scriptable via the admin HTTP API, so promoting a version can be automated. Caveats worth budgeting for: Projects is off by default and labelled preview (`projects: { enabled: false }`; enable via settings or `NODE_RED_ENABLE_PROJECTS=true`); and the git-trackable artifact is the flow JSON itself, which carries node `x`/`y` canvas coordinates and generated ids, so diffs are noisier than a prompt file would be. There is no separate prompt DSL or prompt-object export \u2014 `flows.json` is the DSL.",
 "otel_export": "None. There is no OpenTelemetry support anywhere in the tree \u2014 my grep for `opentelemetry|otlp|traceparent` across the packages returned nothing outside built editor assets. No OTLP exporter, no span model, and consequently no `GENERATION` spans, no prompt/completion payload capture, no token or cost attributes. Node-RED's observability is a different genre: a node-status/debug-sidebar model plus `RED.log` and the `RED.hooks` message-flow hooks, which give us per-message visibility in-process but nothing that speaks OTel. **It cannot retire Langfuse.** Langfuse would sit beside it, and we would have to author the instrumentation ourselves \u2014 the practical shape being a host-side `postDeliver`/`onSend` hook or a wrapper around the OpenAI node's chunk stream that opens and closes spans, which is roughly the work we already did by hand. Concretely: adopting Node-RED buys prompt-and-tool versioning in git (partially replacing the versioning half of the current prompt service) while *losing* every native `gen_ai.*` attribute the AI SDK gives us today, including `gen_ai.client.operation.time_to_first_chunk`, which our own latency work depends on.",
 "accepts_traceparent": "No \u2014 and there is no streaming endpoint to accept it on in the first place. Nothing in the codebase reads, propagates or emits `traceparent` (zero grep hits), and Node-RED has no span model to continue a trace into (see otel_export). Mechanically an inbound header does reach flow code: the `http in` node exposes the Express request as `msg.req`, so a Function node could read `msg.req.headers.traceparent` and we could thread it manually. But nothing does so automatically, and the incremental transports are worse off than that \u2014 `websocket out` frames carry only `msg.payload`, and the in-process path carries a plain object. For our architecture the question largely dissolves: because Node-RED would run inside our own process, the ambient OTel context our service already has is available to any host-side hook we register, so a migration keeps one trace *if we do the joining ourselves*. It would not happen for free, and any out-of-process deployment would produce two disconnected trees.",
 "prompt_prefix_stability": "Byte-stable by construction, because Node-RED contributes nothing to the prompt. Verified in `node.js` of the OpenAI node: the request body is read verbatim from a message property \u2014 `payload = RED.util.getMessageProperty(msg, propertyPath)` (default `msg.payload`) \u2014 and passed straight to `openai.chat.completions.create(params.payload)` with no wrapper, no injected preamble, no run identifier, and no reordering. Tool definitions are just a literal array inside that payload, so their order is whatever the flow author wrote; nothing sorts or regenerates them per run. If the system prompt comes from static text in a Template node, the prefix is identical bytes on every turn and provider prompt caching works normally \u2014 a genuine advantage over frameworks that rebuild the prefix per run. The corollary is that stability becomes entirely our responsibility: the moment a flow composes the prompt from `flow`/`global` context (conversation history, a memory block), cache-prefix discipline is on the flow author, and Node-RED offers no guard rail, no cache-breakpoint concept, and no warning. Note also that editing the prompt requires a Deploy, which restarts the affected nodes \u2014 that changes the prompt bytes deliberately and invalidates the cache once, as expected; there is no live ~20s-TTL prompt refresh like the current setup has.",
 "owns_history": "Node-RED owns nothing \u2014 there is no conversation-memory abstraction in the product at all. No session store keyed by a Node-RED-generated id, no thread object, no automatic history append. What exists is generic key/value context at three scopes (`node`, `flow`, `global`), exposed to Function nodes via `context.get/set`, `flow.get/set`, `global.get/set` (`10-function.js` lines 288-322), in-memory by default with pluggable persistent stores. So a flow that wants multi-turn conversation must explicitly read an array out of context, append the user turn, send the whole `messages` array to the model, and write the assistant turn back \u2014 full history re-sent per turn, by our own code. The alternative is to push ownership to the provider: the OpenAI node exposes the Responses and Conversations APIs (`src/responses/`, `src/conversations/`), so `previous_response_id` or a conversation id can carry state server-side and only the new turn is sent. Either way this is good news for the drift failure mode you flagged \u2014 there is no second history to diverge from ours, because Node-RED never creates one. The cost is that everything our current memory layer does we would rebuild as flow logic, and the uncapped-memory-block problem we already measured would follow us unless the flow caps it.",
 "embeddable_in_node": "Yes \u2014 in-process as an imported library, which is the single best structural fit here. Package `node-red` (v5.0.7, `\"main\": \"lib/red.js\"`), so `require(\"node-red\")` gets the embed surface directly. The documented pattern from https://nodered.org/docs/user-guide/runtime/runtime/embedding is `RED.init(server, settings)` then mounting `app.use(settings.httpAdminRoot, RED.httpAdmin)` and `app.use(settings.httpNodeRoot, RED.httpNode)` on our existing Express app, and `RED.start()`. Reading `lib/red.js` directly: `init(httpServer, userSettings)` accepts our HTTP server (and tolerates `null` for a server-less runtime, with `httpAdminRoot: false` disabling the editor API entirely \u2014 useful for production versus authoring), `start()` returns a Promise, and the module also exports `log`, `util`, `events`, `hooks`, `settings`, `version` and an undocumented `nodes` accessor. Because it is the same process and event loop, a Function node's `node.send()` chunk reaches our voice channel's async-iterable consumer with no serialisation and no network hop \u2014 exactly the seam we want. Three caveats from the source and docs: Node >= 22.9 is hard-enforced in the CLI entry point; when embedding, `settings.js` is bypassed and `uiHost`, `uiPort`, `httpAdminAuth`, `httpNodeAuth`, `httpStatic`, `httpStaticAuth` and `https` are all ignored, so securing the editor is entirely on our Express config (which matters a great deal given sandbox_risk); and the `stop()` docstring warns \"Once called, Node-RED should not be restarted until the Node.JS process is restarted\" \u2014 so no in-process runtime recycling.",
 "visual_editing": "Best-in-class among the candidates, with one real gap. Editing a prompt is direct: open the flow, click the Template or Change node holding the system text, type, Deploy \u2014 no code, no redeploy of our service, and the change is live in seconds. Wiring a tool is equally native and is the thing almost nothing else in open source does: drag a Function node onto the canvas, type the implementation in the browser editor, connect a wire, Deploy. Node-RED's canvas is mature in the ways that matter for a non-engineer \u2014 a searchable palette, per-node help in the sidebar, live node status badges, a debug sidebar showing real messages, subflows for reuse, and partial deploys that restart only modified nodes. The gap is that everything LLM-shaped is unopinionated. There is no \"agent\" node, no prompt editor with variables, no tool-schema form; a caller-facing turn is assembled from a generic OpenAI API node plus switches, templates and Function nodes, and the JSON payload for a tool-calling request has to be hand-built. So a non-engineer could confidently *edit* a prompt and *tweak* an existing tool, but standing up the orchestration in the first place is engineering work on a general-purpose dataflow canvas rather than filling in an agent builder.",
 "maturity": "Very mature, and actively maintained on two lines. 23,654 stars, 3,886 forks, not archived, last push 2026-09-09. Releases are frequent and dual-track: 5.0.7 on 2026-09-08 (with 5.0.6 and 5.0.5 both on 2026-09-01) alongside a maintained 4.x line \u2014 4.1.15 on 2026-09-09 and 4.1.14 on 2026-09-01. 93 commits in the last 90 days. Governance is an OpenJS Foundation project with named long-term maintainers (Nick O'Leary, Dave Conway-Jones) rather than a single-vendor repo, and a documented security policy with OpenJS CNA escalation. Container images are published by the project itself from `node-red/node-red-docker` (540 stars, pushed 2026-09-09, tracking the release same-day) as `nodered/node-red`. Only two CVEs ever filed, both patched in 1.2.8. The maturity asymmetry is the part to weigh: the *platform* is a decade-old, foundation-governed project, while the *AI capability* we would depend on is `@inductiv/node-red-openai-api`, a single-maintainer MIT package (v6.40.0, tracking `openai` SDK ~6.40, requires Node-RED >= 3.0) whose streaming code I verified by hand. The other AI packages I found are editor-sidebar assistants, not runtime nodes: `node-red-contrib-ai-flow-builder` and `@background404/node-red-contrib-llm-plugin` both stream into a chat sidebar for flow authoring, which is irrelevant to a caller-facing turn.",
 "fit_verdict": "Passes all three hard requirements and is the clear leader on requirement 3 and on in-process fit \u2014 but it is a dataflow canvas we would build an agent on, not an agent builder, and it cannot retire Langfuse. Requirement 1: unqualified pass, Apache-2.0, no gate, official images, no control plane. Requirement 3: the strongest pass in the field \u2014 the Function node is a real implementation typed into the browser and executed by `vm` in our process, with `exec` and per-node npm modules available on top. Requirement 2: pass, with the caveat that the proof lives in a third-party node rather than core. `for await (const chunk of response) { _node.send(newMsg) }` in `@inductiv/node-red-openai-api` puts one Node-RED message on the wire per OpenAI SSE delta, and because we embed, each of those reaches our voice channel's async-iterable consumer with no network hop \u2014 a very clean feed into ConversationRelay. Two things temper the recommendation. First, we inherit no agent: no tool-call loop, no memory, no prompt abstraction, zero LLM nodes in core. Streaming through a tool call works, but as a loop we wire on the canvas ourselves. That is less \"replace the hand-written agent\" and more \"re-express it visually\" \u2014 the win being that the prompt and the tool wiring then live in one git-versioned `flows.json` that a Deploy activates without touching our service. Second, observability regresses: no OTel, no `GENERATION` spans, no `gen_ai.client.operation.time_to_first_chunk`, so Langfuse stays and we hand-instrument what the AI SDK gives us free today. Prompt-prefix stability and history ownership both come out *favourably* precisely because Node-RED does nothing \u2014 no per-run prefix rebuild, no shadow conversation store to drift from ours. Recommendation: strongest candidate for requirement 3 and for the in-process seam; treat the editor as privileged (auth, non-public path) and budget for building the agent loop and the tracing.",
 "disqualifiers": [],

  "https://nodered.org/docs/user-guide/projects/ \u2014 projects 'backed by a Git repository, meaning all of the files are fully version controlled'; commit history and per-file diffs; no rebase or force-push in the editor; preview feature requiring settings or NODE_RED_ENABLE_PROJECTS=true",
  "GitHub API /repos/node-red/node-red \u2014 23654 stars, 3886 forks, pushed 2026-09-09, spdx Apache-2.0, not archived; releases 5.0.7 (2026-09-08) and 4.1.15 (2026-09-09); 93 commits since 2026-06-16",
  "GitHub API /repos/node-red/node-red/security-advisories \u2014 CVE-2021-21298 (GHSA-m33v-338h-4v9f, high, path traversal via Projects API) and CVE-2021-21297 (GHSA-xp9c-82x8-7f67, medium, prototype pollution via admin API), both patched in 1.2.8; no Function-node sandbox-escape advisory",
  "GitHub API /repos/node-red/node-red-docker \u2014 project-published container source, 540 stars, pushed 2026-09-09",
  "https://flows.nodered.org/node/node-red-contrib-ai-flow-builder and https://github.com/404background/node-red-contrib-llm-plugin \u2014 editor-sidebar LLM assistants for flow authoring, not runtime nodes"
 ],
 "unverified": [
  "End-to-end token streaming into ConversationRelay was NOT run. I verified the emission code by reading source (per-chunk _node.send) and the host-side receipt surface by reading the exported hooks API, but I did not stand up an embedded Node-RED, wire an OpenAI node with stream:true, and observe chunks arriving at a voice-channel consumer. The chunk-to-caller latency added by Node-RED's message routing and cloning is therefore unmeasured.",
  "Whether Node-RED's per-message overhead (RED.util.cloneMessage, hook triggering, async delivery via Flow.js) is negligible at token cadence. A streamed turn produces one full Node-RED message per delta, each carrying a spread copy of the inbound msg ({ ...parameters.msg, payload: chunk }) \u2014 that is a much higher message rate than typical Node-RED workloads and I did not benchmark it. nodeMessageBufferMaxLength exists as a setting and its interaction with a token-rate burst is unexamined.",
  "Message ORDERING under async delivery. Flow.js triggers postDeliver and notes messages are 'dispatched to be delivered asynchronously (unless the sync delivery flag is set)'. I did not determine whether rapid successive node.send calls are guaranteed to arrive at the downstream node in emission order, which for speech synthesis is not optional. The sync-delivery flag was noted in comments but its setting name, default, and effect were not traced.",
  "Barge-in / cancellation. Nothing was verified about aborting an in-flight streamed turn. The OpenAI node's registerCleanupHandler exists and the openai SDK stream is abortable in principle, but there is no visible per-message cancel path from a flow, and how a Twilio interrupt would stop mid-stream _node.send calls is unknown.",
  "Whether a tool-calling agent loop actually works end to end on the canvas, including streaming text before and after the tool call in one caller turn. I verified the primitives (per-chunk send, link call, Function-node tools) but built and ran nothing; the assertion that streaming survives a tool call is an inference from the code, not a measurement.",
  "Whether any contrib node or plugin provides OTel/OTLP export, GENERATION spans, or traceparent propagation. I confirmed absence in core by grep; I did not survey the ~5000-package flow library for an observability node.",
  "Whether any contrib node provides an SSE-capable http response (res.write / text/event-stream), which would give a network-transport streaming option. Core cannot; the ecosystem was not searched for one.",
  "Exact isolation semantics of RED.import in the Function node \u2014 whether an editor-specified module string can reference an arbitrary npm package or filesystem path, whether Node-RED installs it on demand, and therefore how wide the browser-authored code path really is. I read the call site but not RED.import's implementation.",
  "Whether the Function node's vm context is escapable in practice on current Node (the classic this.constructor.constructor('return process') shape). I asserted vm is not a security boundary as a general property and grounded the risk in the exec node and functionExternalModules, both designed behaviour; I did not attempt or find a documented escape for this specific sandbox.",
  "flows.json diff quality for prompt review \u2014 how noisy a prompt-only change looks in git once node coordinates and generated ids are included, and whether the Projects feature normalises or pretty-prints the JSON to keep diffs readable. Inferred from the format, not observed on a real commit.",
  "Whether the admin API POST /flows can activate a specific committed version without an editor Deploy, and whether credentials (flows_cred.json, encrypted) round-trip cleanly across a git-based rollback. Both are load-bearing for a promote/rollback workflow and neither was tested.",
  "Live prompt editing latency and behaviour \u2014 whether a Deploy restarts only modified nodes cleanly mid-call, and what happens to an in-flight streamed turn when a Deploy lands. Partial deploy is documented as a feature; its effect on an active WebSocket-backed turn is unverified.",
  "The @inductiv/node-red-openai-api test suite was not run and its features/ Cucumber specs were not read, so the streaming behaviour is verified by code reading only. Its bundled lib.js is a build artifact of the openai SDK and I did not verify the bundle matches the upstream SDK version claimed.",
  "Whether functionExternalModules defaults to true in the official Docker image and when embedding, or only in the shipped settings.js template. I read the value from packages/node_modules/node-red/settings.js; the embedded path bypasses settings.js entirely, so the effective default under embedding is unconfirmed."
 ]
}
```

### verdict refuted=True
CLAIM: Node-RED is licensed Apache-2.0 (OSI approved), self-hosted commercial internal use of the needed capabilities is permitted with nothing behind a paid tier; the root LICENSE is the verbatim Apache License 2.0 and GitHub metadata agrees; the only restricting clauses are the standard Apache ones; every source file read carries a matching Apache-2.0 header and no file disagreed; @inductiv/node-red-openai-api is separately MIT (Allan Bunch 2023); zero hits for enterprise/license key/premium/paid tier; no open-core split; red.js requires only runtime and editor-api; Projects is gated by a settings flag (projects: { enabled: false } in default settings.js, or NODE_RED_ENABLE_PROJECTS=true) and functionExternalModules ships true.

CORRECTED: The licensing conclusion holds; four supporting assertions are wrong or overstated.

Confirmed as stated: `github.com/node-red/node-red` root `LICENSE` is Apache License 2.0 terms, `package.json` (node-red 5.0.7) says `"license": "Apache-2.0"`, and GitHub metadata returns `license.spdx_id == "Apache-2.0"`. Self-hosted commercial internal use is permitted with no notice obligation (Apache §4 conditions attach only to redistribution), and nothing in the core is behind a paid tier — GitHub code search over `repo:node-red/node-red` for `enterprise`, `premium` and `license key` returns `total_count: 0` for each. `@inductiv/node-red-openai-api` (latest 6.40.0, published 2026-06-16) is MIT: the registry `license` field is `MIT`, `allanbunch/node-red-openai-api` GitHub metadata is `MIT`, and its `LICENSE` opens `# Copyright 2023 Allan Bunch` followed by the standard MIT grant. FlowFuse is indeed a separate commercial product not present in this repo. `editorTheme.projects.enabled: false` and `functionExternalModules: true` are both confirmed in the default `settings.js`.

Corrections:

1. The LICENSE is NOT verbatim. It prepends `Copyright OpenJS Foundation and other contributors, https://openjsf.org/` and OMITS the entire `APPENDIX: How to apply the Apache License to your work` section — 178 lines against the canonical 202 from apache.org, with the diff being exactly the appendix. The Terms and Conditions (§1–§9 through `END OF TERMS AND CONDITIONS`) are verbatim. There is also no `NOTICE` file at the repo root (404), so the "NOTICE retention" clause has nothing to retain.

2. "Every source file carries the matching Apache-2.0 header. No file disagreed" and "the only restricting clauses are the standard Apache ones" are false for the repo as shipped. `packages/node_modules/@node-red/editor-client/src/vendor/` contains `ace`, `d3`, `font-awesome`, `jquery`, `jsonata` and `monaco`. `vendor/ace/LICENSE` is BSD-3-Clause (`Copyright (c) 2010, Ajax.org B.V.`) and carries a no-endorsement clause — "Neither the name of Ajax.org B.V. nor the names of its contributors may be used to endorse or promote products derived from this software" — which is not an Apache clause. `vendor/font-awesome/css/font-awesome.min.css` declares `License - http://fontawesome.io/license (Font: SIL OFL 1.1, CSS: MIT License)`; `vendor/jquery/js/jquery-3.7.1.min.js` is jQuery under MIT. All permissive, so the practical answer is unchanged, but the licence picture is Apache-2.0 plus vendored BSD-3-Clause / MIT / SIL-OFL-1.1, not Apache-only.

3. `NODE_RED_ENABLE_PROJECTS=true` does NOT work on a plain self-hosted npm install. GitHub code search across `org:node-red` finds it in only 8 places, none of them the runtime: `node-red/node-red` `scripts/dev.js`, five files in `node-red/node-red-docker` (e.g. `.docker/Dockerfile.debian` line 96, `# ENV NODE_RED_ENABLE_PROJECTS=true`), and two docs pages. The core default `settings.js` reads no env var for it — enabling Projects outside the Docker image means editing `settings.js`. Also, the flag is `editorTheme.projects.enabled`, not top-level `projects`. The "in preview mode" quote is real but stale: `docs/user-guide/projects/index.md` says "In the 0.18 release, the projects feature is in preview mode", written for 0.18 while current is 5.0.7.

4. `packages/node_modules/node-red/lib/red.js` requires three internal packages, not two: `@node-red/runtime`, `@node-red/util` and `@node-red/editor-api`. All are Apache-2.0 in-repo, so the "no enterprise middleware imported at boot" conclusion stands.

REASONING: The licence question itself survives scrutiny — Apache-2.0 in the LICENSE file, in package.json, and in GitHub's metadata; no gated tier anywhere in the core; the streaming node genuinely MIT. But the claim was audited on specifics, and four of them do not survive. The LICENSE is not verbatim (the Apache appendix is absent and a copyright line is prepended). The sweeping "no file disagreed" collapses the moment you look at the vendored editor assets, which include a BSD-3-Clause no-endorsement clause and SIL-OFL fonts — extra clauses beyond Apache, harmless for internal use but not "only the standard Apache ones". The NODE_RED_ENABLE_PROJECTS route is a Docker-image convenience, absent from the runtime a self-hoster would install from npm, and the flag path is editorTheme.projects.enabled. red.js requires three packages, not two. Under the stated rule that overstated counts as refuted, refuted=true with the narrower truth above. I did not evaluate streaming-through-an-agent-loop capability, which this claim did not assert.

### verdict refuted=True
CLAIM: Node-RED allows tool definitions AND their implementations to be authored inside the builder rather than in our application source — via the Function node's vm-executed browser-authored JavaScript, per-node npm modules (functionExternalModules), and the exec node; and node.send() is the same primitive that carries streamed chunks.

CORRECTED: Node-RED lets an author write an arbitrary JavaScript *implementation* in the browser — that half is real, correctly sourced, and Apache-2.0 with no tier gate. It does NOT let anyone author a *tool definition*, and three of the claim's supporting statements are wrong or unsourced.

VERIFIED AS STATED (master @ d9644c41):
- LICENSE is Apache-2.0, OpenJS Foundation. No cloud/enterprise gate on any mechanism below.
- `core/function/10-function.js`: `node.script = new vm.Script(functionText, createVMOpt(node, ""))` at line 457, `node.script.runInContext(context, opts)` at line 504 (inside `processMessage`). Node's own `vm`, in-process, same event loop and heap. The claim's "lines 426-504" brackets this correctly.
- Sandbox literal `var sandbox = {` begins line 233 and closes ~line 372 (`clearInterval` at 365). Contents are exactly as claimed: `console`, `util`, `Buffer`, `URL`, `URLSearchParams`, `Date`, `RED.util` spread with `getSetting` neutered, `context`/`flow`/`global`, `env.get`, wrapped `setTimeout`/`setInterval`/`clearTimeout`/`clearInterval`, and a `__node__` façade projected into the user's `node` object with `send`, `status`, `error`, `log`, `warn`, `debug`, `trace`, `done`, `on`, `linkcall`.
- `node.on("input", ...)` is blocked: `if (arguments[0] === "input") throw new Error(RED._("function.error.inputListener"))` (line 273). Other events pass through.
- `fetch` is genuinely absent. I ran `new vm.Script('typeof fetch').runInContext(vm.createContext({console}))` on Node v24.18.0 → `undefined`. Also `require` and `process` → `undefined`.
- `functionExternalModules: true` IS uncommented in the shipped template `packages/node_modules/node-red/settings.js` line 526, and `externalModules.modules.allowInstall` defaults to `true` (`10-function.html` line 184: `RED.settings.get('externalModules.modules.allowInstall', true)`; mirrored in `registry/lib/externalModules.js` lines 67-73). `registerType` also declares `functionExternalModules: { value: true }` (line 613), and the runtime gate only trips on an explicit `=== false` (line 112).
- exec node: `require('child_process').spawn` and `.exec` at `90-exec.js` lines 19-20, invoked at 97/99 (spawn) and 158 (exec), with `cmd`/`append`/`useSpawn`/`timer` all read from the node config. Arbitrary shell from the UI, confirmed.

WHERE IT IS OVERSTATED:

1. There is no tool-definition mechanism in core Node-RED at all. I enumerated all 37 core nodes: common (junction, inject, debug, complete, catch, status, link, comment, global-config, unknown), function (function, switch, change, range, template, delay, trigger, exec, rbe), network (tls, httpproxy, mqtt, httpin, httprequest, websocket, tcpin, udp), parsers (CSV/HTML/JSON/XML/YAML), sequence (split, sort, batch), storage (file, watch). Zero LLM, agent, or tool nodes. The node-red GitHub org's 100 repos contain no AI/agent/MCP project. Surfacing a name + description + JSON input schema to a model therefore requires either a third-party contrib package — the largest Node-RED MCP repo I found has 39 stars, the rest 2-15, none org-maintained — or an adapter written in your own application source. So the claim's own escape condition ("the mechanism requires writing code in our own application after all") applies to the definition half.

2. "a browser-authored tool can `require` real libraries" is wrong as stated. `require` is not in the sandbox (empirically undefined). Modules are declared in a separate Setup-tab list (`node.libs`, each entry bound to a sandbox variable name) and loaded by dynamic ESM `import()` — `registry/lib/externalModules.js` line 119/136 `return import(moduleUrl)`. Per PR #2873 (merged 2021-03-01, the feature's origin): modules install only into `<userDir>/externalModules` with its own second `package.json`, "the install must complete before the flows will be started", and "if the install fails, or the module is not allowed to be installed, the flows will not start" — a bad module name in one Function node blocks the entire flow set, not just that node. Also `RED.import` rejects a variable name that collides with any existing sandbox key or `node` (line ~390).

3. "node.send() inside it is the same primitive that carries streamed chunks" is unsourced and I found no primary evidence for it. There is no streaming, chunk, or delta concept anywhere in core Node-RED, and no model node to stream from. `node.send()` emits whole messages on wires; whether any of that reaches a client as incremental deltas depends entirely on third-party nodes the claim does not name.

4. "a non-engineer-adjacent author can write a complete tool implementation in the builder" is the conclusion the evidence contradicts rather than supports. What was proven is that arbitrary JavaScript can be typed into a browser code editor and stored as a string property in `flows.json`. That is the same skill as editing application source, minus type-checking, tests, and code review, and with errors surfacing as `vm` stack traces that the runtime has to hand-repair (`updateErrorInfo` regexes the stack and subtracts one for the wrapper line; the `.catch` at line 523 slices off `at ContextifyScript.Script.runInContext`).

Also worth recording as a caveat rather than a refutation: the sandbox is not a security boundary, and Node-RED does not claim it is. With `functionExternalModules` default-true plus the exec node, anyone with editor access has host shell equivalence.

REASONING: I read the primary sources rather than docs. The mechanics in the claim are unusually accurate — line numbers, sandbox inventory, the `vm.Script`/`runInContext` pair, the exec node, and the default settings all verified against master, and I confirmed the `fetch` absence empirically on Node 24 instead of trusting the assertion. Apache-2.0 licence means no tier gate, so that refutation route is closed.

It fails on scope and on three specific statements. The claim is offered as evidence about a "Node-RED agent builder", but core Node-RED has no agent: 37 core nodes across six categories, none of them LLM- or tool-related, and no AI repo in the org. So the claim proves "you can author arbitrary code in the browser" and then labels it "you can author a tool" — the definition side, which is the part that would otherwise live in our application source, has no first-party mechanism and falls back to either our own adapter code or a sub-40-star contrib package.

Three narrower errors: `require` is not available in the function body (modules go through a declarative Setup list and dynamic `import()`, installed to a separate `externalModules` dir, with a failed install blocking all flows per PR #2873); the streaming-primitive sentence has no source in core and there is no streaming concept to attach it to; and the "non-engineer-adjacent author" conclusion describes typing JavaScript into a textarea, which is engineering with fewer safety nets, not less of it.

Per the instruction that overstated counts as refuted, refuted=true.

### verdict refuted=True
CLAIM: Node-RED streams token-level model output incrementally during an agent turn that includes tool calls, via repeated node.send() per model chunk out of @inductiv/node-red-openai-api, received in-process by an embedding host through functionGlobalContext or RED.hooks; websocket out emits one WS frame per message, and core http response cannot stream.

CORRECTED: Overstated in attribution and in how much we inherit. Every code quote in the claim is accurate — I verified them against the published tarballs — but the capability is not Node-RED's, and three separate pieces of our own application code are load-bearing, not one adapter.

WHAT VERIFIED EXACTLY AS STATED:
- `@inductiv/node-red-openai-api@6.40.0`, `package/src/chat/methods.js` lines 8-22: `createChatCompletion` — `if (params.payload.stream) { ... for await (const chunk of response) { if (typeof chunk === "object") { const newMsg = { ...parameters.msg, payload: chunk }; _node.send(newMsg); } } ... } else { return response; }`. Character-for-character as quoted, and the streaming branch falls off the end returning `undefined`.
- `package/node.js`: `client[serviceName](serviceParametersObject).then((payload) => { if (payload !== undefined) { msg.payload = payload; node.send(msg); node.status({}); } })` — so no buffered duplicate. Confirmed.
- `src/responses/methods.js` line 163-176 `streamResponse` has the same loop; `src/runs/methods.js` has three (lines 14, 46, 98); `src/images/methods.js` line 25.
- Licence is MIT (`package/LICENSE`, "Copyright 2023 Allan Bunch", full permission grant). Nothing here is gated to a cloud or paid tier. No enterprise edition involved.
- `node-red@5.0.7`, `lib/red.js` line 181: `hooks: runtime.hooks` with the doc-comment "Runtime hooks engine / @see @node-red/runtime_hooks". `@node-red/util@5.0.7` `lib/hooks.js` lines 3-18 `VALID_HOOKS` = onSend, preRoute, preDeliver, postDeliver, onReceive, postReceive, onComplete (+4 install hooks); lines 43-44 carry the exact sentence quoted about onSend receiving "an array of `SendEvent` objects. The messages inside these objects are exactly what the node has passed to `node.send`"; `module.exports = { has, clear, add, remove, trigger }`.
- `@node-red/runtime@5.0.7` `lib/flows/Flow.js`: `hooks.trigger("onSend", ...)` at 791, `preRoute` 805, `preDeliver` 833, `postDeliver` 846.
- `@node-red/nodes@5.0.7` `core/network/22-websocket.js` 428-457: one `reply(msg._session.id, payload)` or `broadcast(payload)` per input message — frame per message confirmed.
- `core/network/21-httpin.js` `HTTPOut` 418-439: `Buffer.byteLength`, `set('content-length', len)`, `status(statusCode).send(msg.payload)`. No `res.write`, no `flushHeaders`, no `text/event-stream` in the file. `21-httprequest.js` line 233 sets `opts.responseType = 'buffer'` unconditionally.
- Core has zero LLM nodes. `core/` is exactly `common function network parsers sequence storage`, and a case-insensitive grep for `openai|anthropic|llm|chatcompletion` across all of it returns nothing in 5.0.7.

WHERE IT IS TOO STRONG:
1. Attribution. "Node-RED streams token-level model output" is false of Node-RED. Core ships no LLM node; the whole capability is one third-party package by one maintainer. Its issue tracker underlines the gap: issue #39 "Support for tools / MCP" (2026-03-08) was closed the same day and LOCKED with no public answer, and #45, which asks precisely how an AI step triggers an action, was closed with no reply. There is no primary source in which the maintainer commits to tool-call support.
2. "Requires writing code in our own application after all" holds three times over, not once. (a) With `stream: true` the streaming branch returns `undefined`, so we never receive an assembled assistant message — for a second turn we must accumulate the message history, including `tool_calls`, ourselves from raw chunks. (b) Tool-call arguments arrive as `delta.tool_calls[].function.arguments` fragments; nothing in the node or in core reassembles them, so argument concatenation and JSON parsing is ours. (c) The host relay/adapter is ours. The claim treats (c) as trivial and does not mention (a) or (b), which are the parts that make a tool-call turn work.
3. "Synchronously" is right about the hook, wrong about the flow. `Flow.js` line 30 `let asyncMessageDelivery = true` and line 838-839 `if (asyncMessageDelivery) { setImmediate(...) }` — default delivery to the next node is a macrotask hop (`runtimeSyncDelivery` in settings flips it). Also `onSend` is global: it fires for every send by every node, so filtering on `sendEvent.source.node.id` is required.
4. "So there is no SSE endpoint" is wrong. The `http response` NODE cannot stream, as stated, but the raw Express response is reachable at `msg.res._res` from a Function node, and `res.writeHead({'Content-Type':'text/event-stream'}) / res.flushHeaders() / res.write("data: ...\n\n")` is the standard Node-RED SSE pattern (and there is a contrib node, `@tq-bit/node-red-contrib-server-sent-events`, that packages it). Note `createResponseWrapper` (21-httpin.js 198-236) wraps only append/attachment/cookie/clearCookie/download/end/format/get/json/jsonp/links/location/redirect/render/send/sendfile/sendFile/sendStatus/set/status/type/vary — not `write` — and every wrapped call emits a `deprecated-call` warning, so the SSE path deliberately goes around the wrapper to a private field.
5. "Identical loops" is loose. `src/runs/methods.js` sends `payload: chunk.data`, not `chunk`, and `src/images/methods.js` sends `payload: event`. Chunk shape differs by endpoint.
6. Frame parsing: confirmed as the claim's caveat (d) says. `RED.util.ensureString` (util.js 59-68) JSON.stringifies an object, so a WS frame is bare JSON with no `data:` prefix, no `\n\n`, no `[DONE]`. Additionally `22-websocket.js` guards with `if (payload)`, so a falsy payload is silently dropped.

Net: the per-chunk emission mechanism is real, MIT-licensed, and correctly quoted. What is overstated is that this is a Node-RED capability and that a tool-call turn survives it for free. A truthful statement is: a third-party MIT node emits each OpenAI SDK chunk as its own Node-RED message; an embedding host can receive each one in-process via the exported `RED.hooks.add("onSend", ...)` or a `functionGlobalContext` relay; and everything that makes those chunks into an agent turn — tool-argument reassembly, message-history accumulation, the loop itself, and the voice-channel adapter — is code we write.

REASONING: I downloaded and extracted the actual published artifacts rather than reading GitHub HEAD: `npm pack @inductiv/node-red-openai-api` (6.40.0) and `npm pack node-red @node-red/nodes @node-red/util @node-red/runtime` (all 5.0.7). Every quoted snippet in the claim matched the file contents, including the `for await` loop, the `if (payload !== undefined)` guard in node.js, the onSend docstring wording, the hook trigger sites in Flow.js, the websocket reply/broadcast handler, and HTTPOut's content-length-then-send. Licence is MIT and permits the use asserted, so the licence refute-trigger does not fire, and nothing is tier-gated.

Three refute triggers do fire. First, "the mechanism requires writing code in our own application after all": because the streaming branch returns `undefined`, `stream: true` yields no assembled assistant message, so both tool-call argument reassembly and message-history accumulation for the next turn are ours — the claim's caveat (c) mentions the loop but not these two, which are the concrete cost. Second, "works only in a narrower configuration than stated": the capability is a single-maintainer contrib package, and the maintainer never publicly answered the two issues (#39, #45) that ask directly about tool support; #39 is closed and locked with no reply. Third, factual overreach in the caveats themselves: "there is no SSE endpoint" is false, since `msg.res._res.write` is the well-known core SSE route (and `createResponseWrapper`'s explicit method list, which omits `write`, is why that path uses the private field); and "synchronously" contradicts `asyncMessageDelivery = true` plus the `setImmediate` at Flow.js:839.

Per the instruction that overstated counts as refuted, refuted=true. Directionally the claim is sound and unusually well sourced — the correction is about attribution, the async default, the SSE absolutism, and the two unmentioned pieces of our own code.


## VoltAgent

```json
{
 "name": "VoltAgent",
 "repo_url": "https://github.com/VoltAgent/voltagent",
 "license_spdx": "MIT",
 "license_is_osi_approved": true,
 "license_restrictions": "None. The only licence file in the repo is `LICENCE` (British spelling \u2014 note `find -iname 'LICENSE*'` misses it): \"MIT License / Copyright (c) 2025 VoltAgent Inc. ... to deal in the Software without restriction, including without limitation the rights to use, copy, modify, merge, publish, distribute, sublicense, and/or sell copies\". No Commons Clause, no BSL, no field-of-use limit. All 29 workspace packages declare `\"license\": \"MIT\"` (`grep -h '\"license\"' packages/*/package.json` \u2192 29\u00d7 MIT, zero disagreements). Self-hosted commercial internal use is unambiguously permitted. No `ee/` or `enterprise/` directory exists.",
 "enterprise_gated_paths": "No enterprise code path in the repo, and nothing enterprise is imported at boot. The paid gate is a separate hosted product, VoltOps: `website/src/pages/pricing.tsx` lists Developer $0 = \"1 prompt\", \"250 traces/month\", and the Deployment feature matrix row `{ name: \"Self-hosted\", developer: false, core: false, pro: false, enterprise: true }` \u2014 a self-hosted VoltOps is Enterprise-only, and the FAQ text says \"VoltOps Enterprise plan includes self-hosted deployment options\". Two capabilities we would want therefore sit behind the SaaS: the prompt versioning UI (1 prompt free) and the trace UI. The framework requires neither. Checked the boot shape specifically, since round one found a candidate importing enterprise middleware unconditionally: `NodeVoltAgentObservability.setupProcessors` installs a `LazyRemoteExportProcessor` unconditionally, but `tryInitialize()` returns false when `AgentRegistry.getInstance().getGlobalVoltOpsClient()` is absent and it logs \"Gave up waiting for VoltOpsClient after 5 seconds\" \u2014 no keys, no egress, no phone-home.",
 "self_host": "Full. VoltAgent is a library you install into your own Node process, not a server product \u2014 there is nothing to self-host beyond our own container. `new VoltAgent({ agents, server: honoServer() })` starts a Hono HTTP server (default :3141, Swagger UI at `/ui`) inside our process; memory and observability storage are pluggable local adapters (`InMemoryStorageAdapter`, `@voltagent/libsql` file-backed SQLite, `@voltagent/postgres`, `@voltagent/supabase`). It drops into our existing docker-compose as an import in the agent service. No VoltAgent-published image, no control plane, and `@voltagent/core` 2.10.0 has zero hosted runtime dependency.",
 "streams_token_level": true,
 "streaming_transport": "Two surfaces. In process: `agent.streamText(input, options)` returns an object exposing `textStream: AsyncIterableStream<string>` and `fullStream: AsyncIterable<VoltAgentTextStreamPart>` (declared `packages/core/src/agent/agent.ts:700-701`, getters at :2810-2815). That is a bare async iterable of strings \u2014 it feeds a ConversationRelay channel method that consumes `AsyncIterable<string>` with no adapter. Over HTTP: `POST /agents/:id/stream` (`packages/server-core/src/routes/definitions.ts:104-126`, contentType `text/event-stream`), whose handler iterates `fullStream` and writes `data: ${safeStringify(part)}\\n\\n` per part (`packages/server-core/src/handlers/agent.handlers.ts:160-180`). Frames are anonymous SSE `data:` events carrying AI SDK v5 stream parts (`{type:\"text-delta\", ...}`, `{type:\"tool-call\"}`, `{type:\"tool-result\"}`, `{type:\"finish\"}`). A standard SSE client parses them fine; an OpenAI client cannot \u2014 no `event:` name, no `data: [DONE]` sentinel, and the JSON is AI SDK's shape, not `chat.completion.chunk`. There is also `POST /agents/:id/chat` returning `result.toUIMessageStreamResponse()` (AI SDK UI protocol for `useChat`) and `POST /agents/:id/chat/:conversationId/stream` to resume via `@voltagent/resumable-streams`.",
 "streaming_evidence": "Source, not docs. `packages/core/src/agent/agent.ts:2061-2078` calls Vercel AI SDK `streamText({ model, messages, tools, stopWhen: options?.stopWhen ?? this.stopWhen ?? stepCountIs(maxSteps), ... abortSignal: oc.abortController.signal })` and returns its `textStream`/`fullStream` through a wrapper. Because the stop condition is `stepCountIs(maxSteps)` over the AI SDK multi-step loop, text deltas from every step \u2014 including steps after tool calls \u2014 leave the runtime incrementally in the same stream; nothing awaits flow completion. The SSE handler's `for await (const part of fullStream)` loop enqueues each part as it arrives. Guardrail docs corroborate the per-chunk path: `website/docs/guardrails/overview.md:44` \u2014 \"`streamHandler` runs on each chunk as the model streams output\".",
 "streaming_caveats": "Four, all conditional rather than structural. (1) Output guardrails, if configured, interpose per chunk (`packages/core/src/agent/streaming/output-guardrail-stream-runner.ts`); a guardrail implementing only the non-stream `handler` runs after `finish` and \"can override the result that callers see\" \u2014 it changes what the caller already heard rather than buffering ahead of them. Guardrails are opt-in and absent by default. (2) `structuredOutput`/`streamObject` streams partial objects, not token text \u2014 wrong surface for speech. (3) Subagent/supervisor mode routes child output through a `fullStreamWriter` (`agent.ts:2512`); forwarding of subagent deltas into the parent's `textStream` I did not exercise. (4) The resumable-stream variant is Redis/VoltOps-backed and its concurrency is a metered VoltOps quota (\"Resumable Streams (Concurrent)\" 1/100/1,000/Custom in pricing.tsx) \u2014 use the plain in-process path instead. No coalescing window, no post-processing that disables the stream, and no \"terminal node only\" behaviour: VoltAgent's Agent is not a graph, so the graph-runtime buffering failure mode does not apply. Workflow streams (`/workflows/:id/stream`) are event-level, not token-level, but a voice turn would not use them.",
 "tools_in_builder": "No \u2014 and \"definition-only\" understates it: both the schema and the implementation are TypeScript we host. `createTool({ name, description, parameters: z.object({...}), execute: async (args, options) => ... })` (`website/docs/tools/overview.md`), registered by passing `tools: [...]` to `new Agent(...)`. No HTTP route creates or edits an agent, prompt or tool: the full route table (`packages/server-core/src/routes/definitions.ts`) offers `GET /tools` and `POST /tools/:name/execute`, and the execute handler refuses anything without code behind it \u2014 `if (!tool.execute) return { error: \"Tool ${toolName} cannot be executed on the server\" }` (`tool.handlers.ts:167-170`). Mutating routes exist only for memory/conversations, working memory, workspace files, and `POST /updates` (npm package updates). The nearest thing to external tool authoring is MCP: `/mcp/servers/:serverId/tools` lets an agent consume tools from an MCP server, but that implementation lives in the MCP server \u2014 still code we host, just elsewhere. Executor for in-builder code: none exists, because no in-builder code exists. This fails hard requirement 3.",
 "sandbox_risk": "No browser-authored code executes anywhere, because nothing is authored in a browser. The only code-execution surface is the agent-facing Workspace sandbox: an `execute_command` tool backed by `LocalSandbox`, which is `spawn()` from `node:child_process` (`packages/core/src/workspace/sandbox/local.ts:1,548`) \u2014 host-level execution with no isolation unless you opt in via `isolation: { provider: \"sandbox-exec\" | \"bwrap\", allowNetwork: false, readWritePaths: [...] }` (`website/docs/workspaces/sandbox.md`: macOS `sandbox-exec` / Linux bubblewrap; \"If the provider is unavailable or unsupported on the current OS, execution will throw\"). Remote providers are third-party SaaS (`@voltagent/sandbox-e2b`, `-daytona`, `-blaxel`). The whole Workspace API carries a \":::warning Experimental\" banner. We would simply not construct a Workspace, in which case there is no executor at all. I claim no CVE \u2014 I retrieved no advisory for VoltAgent and will not assert one.",
 "prompt_versioning": "Split. Prompt text: yes, with versions, labels, diff and rollback \u2014 but in the hosted VoltOps console, not self-hosted. `website/prompt-engineering-docs/creating-prompts.md`: \"Click **New Version**... Add a commit message describing changes\"; \"Select **Promote to** and choose a label... When you promote a version to a label, that label is automatically removed from any other version. Each label points to exactly one version\"; plus a side-by-side word-level Diff View. `overview.md`: versions are \"immutable once created\", labels `production|staging|development|testing|latest` plus custom. The runtime surface is public and documented, not an internal route: `prompts.getPrompt({ promptName, label, variables })` with `x-public-key`/`x-secret-key`, and REST export at `GET $VOLTAGENT_API_URL/prompts/public/export/markdown`. Git-trackable: genuinely yes \u2014 prompts round-trip as Markdown with frontmatter (`name/description/type/version/labels`), `volt prompts pull|push` implements the sync (`packages/cli/src/commands/prompts.ts`, `pushDiffResults`, `--names/--label/--prompt-version/--clean`), and `@voltagent/core` reads them offline from `.voltagent/prompts` via `VOLTAGENT_PROMPTS_PATH`/`VOLTAGENT_PROMPTS_DIR` (`packages/core/src/voltops/local-prompts.ts`). No DSL beyond `{{variable}}` templating. Tool selection: NOT versioned with the prompt \u2014 a version carries content plus an optional config JSON of model params (`temperature`, `maxTokens`, `stopSequences`, metadata); `grep -n tools packages/core/src/voltops/types.ts` finds nothing, and `tools:` is a constructor argument in our code. Activating a prompt AND its tool set together without redeploy is therefore not achievable. Free tier caps you at 1 prompt.",
 "otel_export": "Native OpenTelemetry throughout, and it sits BESIDE Langfuse rather than replacing it. `VoltAgentObservability` wraps a real `NodeTracerProvider` and accepts arbitrary user processors \u2014 `if (this.config.spanProcessors) processors.push(...)` (`packages/core/src/observability/node/volt-agent-observability.ts:160`) \u2014 so a standard `OTLPTraceExporter` pointed at our Langfuse works. There is also a first-party `@voltagent/langfuse-exporter` (MIT, `createLangfuseSpanProcessor({ publicKey, secretKey, baseUrl })`, `website/docs/observability/langfuse.md`) mapping VoltAgent spans onto Langfuse `generation()` observations carrying `input`/`output` (from `ai.prompt.messages` / `ai.response.text`, `packages/langfuse-exporter/src/exporter.ts:405-432`) and token usage \u2014 payloads are exported, not withheld. VoltAgent's own remote path is OTLP/HTTP JSON to `${baseUrl}/api/public/otel/v1/traces`. The catch to price in: VoltAgent does NOT set `experimental_telemetry` on its `streamText` call (`grep -rn experimental_telemetry packages/` hits only the vercel-ai-exporter README) and emits no `gen_ai.*` attributes at all (`grep -rn gen_ai packages/core/src` \u2192 zero). Adopting it as the runtime loses `gen_ai.client.operation.time_to_first_chunk`, the attribute our latency method depends on. Options spread as `...aiSDKOptions` into `streamText` (agent.ts:2069), so passing `experimental_telemetry` through looks possible at the cost of double instrumentation.",
 "accepts_traceparent": "Over HTTP: NO. `propagation.extract` appears exactly once in the server, in `executeTriggerHandler` (`packages/server-core/src/handlers/trigger.handlers.ts:269`, comment \"Extract trace context from headers\"); `handleStreamText` never touches trace headers \u2014 `processAgentOptions(body, signal, requestHeaders)` forwards headers only to dynamic instructions and tools. `grep -rn traceparent packages/core/src` returns nothing. Worse for the HTTP shape: `TraceContext` deliberately severs any ambient parent \u2014 \"Otherwise, use a clean context (remove any ambient spans) / This ensures agent root spans are truly root\", then `const parentContext = parentSpan ? trace.setSpan(context.active(), parentSpan) : trace.deleteSpan(context.active())` (`packages/core/src/agent/open-telemetry/trace-context.ts:134-139`), attaching the ambient span only as a span *link* (`link.type: \"ambient-parent\"`). An out-of-process migration therefore yields two disconnected trees. In process: YES, via an explicit escape hatch \u2014 call options expose `parentSpan?: Span` and `inheritParentSpan?: boolean` (`agent.ts:905-906`, `types.ts:1130`, passed to the trace context at `agent.ts:4218`), so handing it our `turn.voice` span keeps one trace. That is the only configuration in which the trace survives.",
 "prompt_prefix_stability": "Not stable by default, and the instability sits at the END of the system prompt where it does most damage to a prefix cache. `getSystemMessage` \u2192 `enrichInstructions` (`packages/core/src/agent/agent.ts:5586-5625`) appends, in fixed order: toolkit instructions, an optional markdown line, then `\\n\\nRelevant Context:\\n${retrieverContext}` \u2014 recomputed from the CURRENT user utterance on every turn when a retriever is attached \u2014 then the working-memory block, re-read per turn and growing. Tool definitions come from `prepareTools` over an array we supply, so their order is stable; base instructions are stable when static and re-fetched per run (client-cached) when using `prompts.getPrompt`. No run id is injected into the prefix. Net: with a retriever attached every turn is a cold turn from the first appended byte onward \u2014 structurally the same failure mode as our current uncapped memory block. Mitigation is explicit: `instructions` may return `{ type: \"chat\", messages: [{ role: \"system\", content: ..., providerOptions: { anthropic: { cacheControl: { type: \"ephemeral\", ttl: \"5m\" } } } }] }` (`website/docs/agents/prompts.md`), and for the chat form enrichment is appended to the last system message, so a cache breakpoint can be placed before the volatile tail. This is a read of the concatenation order, not a measurement.",
 "owns_history": "VoltAgent owns it, keyed by its own `userId` + `conversationId`, and re-sends per turn. `memoryManager.saveMessage(oc, message, oc.userId, oc.conversationId)` persists both sides (`agent.ts:3084`, :3538), and each run loads back a window governed by `contextLimit` (`agent.ts:1256`, :1930) plus optional `semanticMemory`. Adapters: in-memory (default), `@voltagent/libsql`, `-postgres`, `-supabase`, `-cloudflare-d1`. We can pass the ConversationRelay call SID as `conversationId` so the keys align, and persistence is suppressible per call (`shouldPersistMemoryForContext`, `conversationPersistence` option) \u2014 but keeping our own transcript alongside means two stores that can drift. There is also a separate \"working memory\" facility whose instructions are injected into the system prompt each turn, and a full read/write memory REST surface (`/api/memory/*`) that the console uses.",
 "embeddable_in_node": "Yes, in process, and this is its strongest fit. `npm i @voltagent/core` (2.10.0, MIT) \u2192 `new Agent({ name, instructions, model: \"openai/gpt-4o-mini\", tools })`, then `const { textStream } = await agent.streamText(input, { parentSpan, abortSignal, conversationId, userId })`. No server, no `VoltAgent({...})` wrapper and no `@voltagent/server-hono` needed to run a turn \u2014 the HTTP server exists to serve the console and REST clients. `abortSignal` reaches the AI SDK call directly, which is what barge-in needs. Because it is a thin layer over the same Vercel AI SDK v5 we already call, the seam is a drop-in: our voice channel method already consumes an async iterable of text.",
 "visual_editing": "Weak for our purpose, and observe-only where it matters. The \"visual console\" is VoltOps, a closed-source SPA hosted at https://console.voltagent.dev whose source is not in this repo (no console package in `packages/`); the browser connects to your local server on :3141, and the docs are explicit that \"No data is sent to or stored on any external servers for this local debugging mode\" (`website/docs/observability/developer-console.md`). What it can EDIT: prompts (in the VoltOps cloud project \u2014 versions, labels, diff, promote), memory and working memory, workspace files; and it can invoke agents and registered tools to test them. What it cannot edit: agents, tool code, tool schemas, or which tools an agent gets \u2014 no route exists for any of that. So a non-engineer could edit a prompt (1 free, then $50/mo) and could not wire a tool. No drag-and-drop or flow canvas: `grep -rin \"no-code|visual builder|drag and drop\" website/docs README.md CONSOLE.md` returns zero hits. The repo's \"AI Agent Engineering Platform\" framing is marketing over a code-first framework.",
 "maturity": "Real but young, and slowing. First npm publish 2025-04-18; latest `@voltagent/core` 2.10.0 published 2026-08-27, which is also the newest commit on `main` (44b4c8e, 2026-08-27 17:36 +0300) \u2014 roughly three weeks stale as of 2026-09-16. 10,631 stars, 30+ contributors, 32 commits in the trailing 90 days (GitHub commit search) across a 29-package Lerna/nx monorepo: small core-team cadence, not a foundation project. 23,532 npm downloads/week for `@voltagent/core`. The project publishes no container images (it is a library; we build our own). Several surfaces we would touch are labelled experimental in their own docs (Workspace filesystem, sandbox, skills). Publisher is VoltAgent Inc., a commercial company monetising the VoltOps SaaS \u2014 the framework is the funnel, which is the usual sustainability question.",
 "fit_verdict": "Strong on the two hard requirements that usually kill candidates, and it fails the third outright. The licence is clean MIT with no gate on anything in the runtime, and token-level streaming is real and structural \u2014 `agent.streamText().textStream` is an async iterable of strings produced by AI SDK v5 under `stopWhen: stepCountIs(maxSteps)`, so deltas flow during tool-calling turns and plug into the ConversationRelay channel seam with zero adaptation. But hard requirement 3 fails: tools are `createTool` + zod + `execute` in TypeScript we host, and no HTTP route or console surface creates or edits one. Prompts are editable outside code only via the hosted VoltOps console (1 prompt free, self-hosted VoltOps is Enterprise-only), so it cannot retire our self-hosted prompt service either \u2014 though `volt prompts pull/push` plus `.voltagent/prompts` gives a genuinely git-trackable prompt workflow, and tool selection is never versioned with a prompt in any case. Two migration costs specific to us: no `gen_ai.*` attributes and no `experimental_telemetry`, so we lose `gen_ai.client.operation.time_to_first_chunk`; and `TraceContext` calls `trace.deleteSpan(context.active())` to force a root span, so the trace stays whole only if we embed in process and pass the `parentSpan` option. Honest read: the best-shaped runtime in the round for voice, and essentially a well-built layer over the AI SDK we already call directly \u2014 it buys memory, tracing, guardrails and prompt fetch, not in-builder authoring. Recommend as a runtime candidate; reject as the builder.",
```
