### verdict refuted=True
CLAIM: VoltAgent allows tool definitions AND their implementations to be authored inside the builder rather than in our application source. Mechanism offered: No — and "definition-only" understates it: both the schema and the implementation are TypeScript we host. createTool({...execute}) registered via tools: [...] on new Agent(...). No HTTP route creates or edits an agent, prompt or tool; the full route table (packages/server-core/src/routes/definitions.ts) offers GET /tools and POST /tools/:name/execute, and the execute handler refuses anything without code behind it (tool.handlers.ts:167-170). Mutating routes exist only for memory/conversations, working memory, workspace files, and POST /updates. Nearest thing to external tool authoring is MCP. No in-builder executor. Fails hard requirement 3.

CORRECTED: The bottom line survives — VoltAgent has no in-builder tool authoring and no executor for builder-authored code, so hard requirement 3 fails — but three of the supporting assertions are wrong or overstated.

1. "Definition-only understates it" is backwards: definition-only tools are first-class in VoltAgent. `execute` is optional on `Tool`; `packages/core/src/tool/index.ts` defines `isClientSide = (): boolean => typeof this.execute !== "function"` with the doc comment "Whether this tool should be executed on the client side. Returns true when no server-side execute handler is provided", `ToolManager.ts` skips such tools ("client side tools don't have execute function"), and `agent.ts` throws `Tool "${target.name}" cannot be executed on the server or has no execute handler.` Provider-defined tools are the same shape — `website/docs/tools/overview.md`: "They are not executable on your server via the usual `Tool.execute` handler — the provider manages them." So a schema you host with an implementation you do not host is supported; the implementation just moves to your browser client or the model provider, never to a builder.

2. The mutating-route enumeration is inaccurate in both directions. All five workspace routes in `definitions.ts` are GET (`getWorkspace`, `/workspace/ls`, `/workspace/read`, `/workspace/skills`, `/workspace/skills/:skillId`) — there are no workspace-file writes, in `definitions.ts` or in `packages/server-hono/src/routes/index.ts`. And the claim omits mutating routes that do exist: `POST /setup-observability`, `POST /mcp/servers/:serverId/logging/level`, `POST /a2a/:serverId`, the five `POST /agents/:id/{text,stream,chat,object,stream-object}` run routes, and `POST /workflows/:id/{execute,stream}` plus suspend/cancel/resume/replay (68 route definitions total).

3. "No HTTP route creates or edits an agent, prompt or tool" is true of the self-hosted server but silently swaps "the builder" for it, and the "nearest thing is MCP" line is therefore wrong. VoltOps Prompt Management is exactly platform-UI authoring outside code: `website/docs/agents/prompts.md` lists it as one of three instruction approaches — "VoltOps Management | Externally managed prompts | Platform-managed", "Prompts are created and versioned in the VoltOps platform, then fetched at runtime" via `prompts.getPrompt({ promptName, label, variables })` with a `VoltOpsClient` keyed on `VOLTAGENT_PUBLIC_KEY`/`VOLTAGENT_SECRET_KEY`, and a comparison table scoring it "Non-technical Editing: Yes", "External Dependency: Yes", "Offline Support: No". So in-builder authoring does exist in VoltAgent — for prompts, in the hosted cloud tier only, never for tool code.

The two quoted primary sources check out verbatim: `tool.handlers.ts` lines 167-170 are `if (!tool.execute) { ... error: \`Tool ${toolName} cannot be executed on the server\`, httpStatus: 400 }`, and `handleListTools` additionally hides implementation-less tools ("Only expose tools that can run server-side").

REASONING: I read the cited files directly rather than the summary. The core negative finding is correct: the 68-entry route table in packages/server-core/src/routes/definitions.ts has no create/update route for agents, prompts or tools, and the /tools/:name/execute handler rejects any tool lacking a hosted `execute`, at exactly the cited lines. But per the instruction that overstated counts as refuted, three supporting claims fail. (a) The strengthening flourish "definition-only understates it" is contradicted by VoltAgent's own client-side-tool support — `Tool.isClientSide()` exists precisely because a tool may be schema-only, and provider-defined tools are executed by the provider; so the framework does separate schema from implementation, just not into a builder. (b) The claim asserts mutating workspace-file routes; every workspace route in both server-core and server-hono is a GET, while several real mutating routes (setup-observability, MCP logging level, a2a, agent-run and workflow-control POSTs) are omitted. (c) The claim's own framing is "authored inside the builder", then argues from the self-hosted HTTP surface; VoltOps — the builder/console — does host prompt content authored in its UI by non-technical users, which makes "the nearest thing to external authoring is MCP" false. None of this rescues tool implementations: there is no route, no storage and no executor for builder-authored tool code, so requirement 3 still fails — the accurate statement is narrower than the one given.

### verdict refuted=True
CLAIM: VoltAgent is licensed MIT (OSI approved), self-hosted commercial internal use is permitted with nothing behind a paid tier, restrictions none; the only licence file is `LICENCE`; all 29 workspace packages declare `"license": "MIT"` with zero disagreements; no `ee/`/`enterprise/` directory; the paid gate is the separate hosted VoltOps SaaS (Developer $0 = 1 prompt / 250 traces/month, Self-hosted row enterprise-only), and at boot `NodeVoltAgentObservability.setupProcessors` installs `LazyRemoteExportProcessor` unconditionally but `tryInitialize()` returns false without a global VoltOpsClient — no keys, no egress, no phone-home.

CORRECTED: The licence substance holds — VoltAgent's only licence file is `LICENCE` (British spelling; `find -iname 'LICENSE*'` does miss it) and it is verbatim MIT, "Copyright (c) 2025 VoltAgent Inc.", with no Commons Clause, no BSL, no field-of-use clause. Repo-wide there is no `ee/` or `enterprise/` directory and no licence-key or entitlement check at all (`grep -rin 'license_key|licenseKey|VOLTAGENT_LICENSE|entitlement'` over `packages/`, `website/src/`, `docs/` returns nothing). Self-hosted commercial internal use of the framework is unambiguously permitted. Three parts of the claim are overstated:

1. Package enumeration. `packages/` holds 34 directories but only 30 `package.json` files; 29 declare `"license": "MIT"` and `packages/e2e` is `"private": true` with no `license` field (the 4 manifest-less dirs are `anthropic-ai`, `google-ai`, `groq-ai` — DEPRECATED.md stubs — and `shared`, which is only `tsup-plugins`). The pnpm workspace is not 29 packages: `pnpm-workspace.yaml` globs `packages/*`, `examples/*` and `examples/**/*`, and 101 manifests repo-wide carry a `license` field, two of which are ISC not MIT (`examples/with-zapier-mcp/package.json:20`, `examples/with-lancedb/package.json:22`). "All 29 workspace packages, zero disagreements" is true only of the `packages/*` glob the grep actually covered. (Both ISC and MIT are permissive, so the conclusion is unaffected.)

2. The boot shape. `LazyRemoteExportProcessor` is not installed unconditionally: `setupProcessors` skips it when `voltOpsSync.sampling.strategy === "never"`, and wraps it in `SamplingWrapperProcessor` for `"ratio"`; at the default `"always"` it is installed. And "no keys, no egress" holds only while no keys exist in the environment — `VoltAgent.autoConfigureVoltOpsClientFromEnv()` silently constructs and registers a global `VoltOpsClient` whenever `VOLTAGENT_PUBLIC_KEY` and `VOLTAGENT_SECRET_KEY` are both present and valid, failing silently at debug level either way, and `packages/server-core` exposes a `POST /setup-observability` route whose documented job is to write those keys into your `.env`. Once a client is registered, egress starts even mid-run: `tryInitialize()` is re-attempted on every `onStart`/`onEnd` long after the 5-second interval is cleared, and the up-to-1000 spans buffered in `pendingSpans` (retained indefinitely, oldest dropped) are flushed to `${baseUrl}/api/public/otel/v1/traces`. The "Gave up waiting for VoltOpsClient after 5 seconds" line is `logger?.debug`, so it is invisible at default log levels. There is a parallel `observability/logs/remote-log-processor.ts` for logs.

3. The gating description is pessimistic in our favour. The pricing quotes are verbatim correct (Developer $0 lists "1 prompt" and "250 traces/month"; the Deployment matrix row is `{ name: "Self-hosted", developer: false, core: false, pro: false, enterprise: true }`; the FAQ says "VoltOps Enterprise plan includes self-hosted deployment options"), but neither capability we want is absent from the MIT code. Prompt versioning has a file-based path in core — `.voltagent/prompts` markdown with gray-matter frontmatter carrying a numeric `version`, overridable via `VOLTAGENT_PROMPTS_PATH`/`VOLTAGENT_PROMPTS_DIR` — and traces are readable locally: `LocalStorageSpanProcessor` over `InMemoryStorageAdapter` (maxSpans 10000) behind documented routes `GET /observability/traces`, `/observability/traces/:traceId`, `/observability/spans/:spanId`, `/observability/logs`, `/observability/status`. What Enterprise buys is the hosted visualiser and prompt registry, not the data. Note the pricing page is marketing copy, not a licence, and the console UI itself is not in this repo.

REASONING: Cloned VoltAgent/voltagent at 44b4c8e (2026-08-27) and read primary sources rather than docs. `LICENCE` is verbatim MIT and is the only licence file at depth 2. No `ee/`/`enterprise/` directory anywhere outside `.git`, and no licence-key/entitlement gate in code — so the licence assertion itself survives. It fails on precision: the package census is 30 manifests (29 MIT + one private, unlicensed `e2e`) inside `packages/`, while the actual pnpm workspace also spans `examples/**`, where two manifests are ISC. The boot claim is also narrower than stated — the lazy remote processor is behind a `sampling.strategy !== "never"` guard, and the "no phone-home" property is a property of the environment, not the code: `autoConfigureVoltOpsClientFromEnv` opts you into export from two env vars with no explicit call, the giveup is logged only at debug, and re-initialisation is retried per span so buffered spans can flush later. Finally, the claim understates the MIT surface: local prompt versioning and local trace read APIs both exist in MIT code, so "two capabilities sit behind the SaaS" should be "two hosted UIs sit behind the SaaS". Per the round-one rule, overstated counts as refuted.

### verdict refuted=True
CLAIM: VoltAgent streams token-level model output incrementally during an agent turn that includes tool calls, via two surfaces: in-process `agent.streamText()` exposing `textStream: AsyncIterableStream<string>` / `fullStream`, and over HTTP `POST /agents/:id/stream` as anonymous SSE `data:` frames carrying AI SDK v5 stream parts; caveats are conditional (opt-in guardrails, structuredOutput, unexercised subagent forwarding, resumable-stream quota) rather than structural.

CORRECTED: The core mechanism is real and MIT-licensed, but the claim is overstated on two points that matter for a voice pipeline, and one of its caveats hides a currently-open defect.

WHAT HOLDS (verified in @voltagent/core 2.10.0 / server-core 2.1.20, cloned at HEAD 2026-09-16):
- Licence: root `LICENCE` is "MIT License, Copyright (c) 2025 VoltAgent Inc."; `core`, `server-core`, `server-hono` and `resumable-streams` each declare `"license": "MIT"`. No tier gate on the streaming path, no hosted dependency — VoltOps observability is optional.
- `packages/core/src/agent/agent.ts:2061` calls AI SDK `streamText({ model, messages, tools, stopWhen: options?.stopWhen ?? this.stopWhen ?? stepCountIs(maxSteps), … abortSignal })`, and the returned wrapper's getters (`textStream` at :2810, `fullStream` at :2813) delegate to the AI SDK result. With no guardrails configured, `applySpeculativeInputGuardrailToTextStream` returns `params.baseStream` unchanged (`streaming/input-guardrail-stream.ts:141-146`), so the default path is the unmodified AI SDK stream — deltas from post-tool-call steps do leave incrementally, with no coalescing.
- `POST /agents/:id/stream` is declared at `server-core/src/routes/definitions.ts:104-129` with `contentType: "text/event-stream"`; `handleStreamText` (`handlers/agent.handlers.ts:129`) does `for await (const part of fullStream)` at :167 and writes `data: ${safeStringify(part)}\n\n` at :169, one frame per part, no batching. Confirmed: no `event:` name, no `data: [DONE]` sentinel, no heartbeat/comment lines. A generic SSE parser works; an OpenAI SDK client does not.
- The guardrail doc line is real: `website/docs/guardrails/overview.md:44` — "`streamHandler` runs on each chunk as the model streams output".
- `POST /agents/:id/chat` returns `result.toUIMessageStreamResponse(...)` (`agent.handlers.ts:336`), and `POST /agents/:id/chat/:conversationId/stream` exists (`definitions.ts:160`).

WHERE IT IS OVERSTATED:
1. SUB-AGENT TEXT IS STRUCTURALLY ABSENT FROM `textStream`, not merely "not exercised". The claim files this as caveat (3). In fact `getGuardrailAwareTextStream()` always returns AI SDK's own `result.textStream`; the sub-agent merge happens *only* on `fullStream`, via `createMergedFullStream` / `oc.systemContext.set("fullStreamWriter", writer)` (`agent.ts:2506-2530`). No configuration puts a child agent's tokens into the parent's `textStream`. So the surface the claim says feeds ConversationRelay with no adapter carries a delegating supervisor's own text only.
2. AND ON `fullStream`, SUB-AGENT `text-delta` IS OFF BY DEFAULT. The default allow-list is hard-coded to `["tool-call", "tool-result", "input-guardrail-blocked"]` at `packages/core/src/agent/subagent/index.ts:627` and `:659`; `website/docs/agents/subagents.md:116` and the table at `:838` mark `text-delta` "❌ NOT included". You must set `fullStreamEventForwarding.types` — i.e. application code after all, for the supervisor case.
3. CAVEAT (1) UNDERSTATES AN OPEN DEFECT. Issue #1415 (open, filed 2026-09-14, still open as of 2026-09-16; PR #1417 "fix(core): close supervisor streams with output guardrails" is OPEN, unmerged) reports that a supervisor agent with `outputGuardrails` makes the SSE stream **never emit `finish` and never close the controller** — the client hangs indefinitely. Reproduced by the reporter on 2.9.2 and 2.10.0, via both `streamText()` and the built-in SSE endpoint. That is a hang, not "it changes what the caller already heard".
4. MINOR CITATION ERRORS. At `agent.ts:700` `textStream` is typed `AIStreamTextResult<TOOLS, any>["textStream"]`, not the literal `AsyncIterableStream<string>` the claim quotes (that literal is at `:726`, on a different type). `fullStream` at `:701` is `AsyncIterable<VoltAgentTextStreamPart<TOOLS>>` = `(TextStreamPart<TOOLS> | InputGuardrailBlockedStreamPart) & VoltAgentStreamMetadata` (`subagent/types.ts:195-199`). The handler loop is at `:167-172`, not 160-180.
5. THE RETURNED PROMISE IS GATED ON THE FIRST TOKEN. `probeStreamStart` (`agent.ts:6163-6208`) tees `fullStream` and awaits the first non-`start` part to decide model fallback, so `await agent.streamText(...)` does not resolve until the model has produced something. Not output buffering, but you cannot attach a consumer before first-token.
6. Frame field naming: the guardrails doc's own example reads `part.delta ?? part.text ?? ""` "with text fallback for compatibility", so a consumer that only reads `.text` off a `text-delta` frame may get `undefined` in this version.

Narrower true statement: on a single agent with its own tools, VoltAgent (MIT, self-hosted) streams token deltas incrementally across the AI SDK multi-step loop, both in-process via `textStream`/`fullStream` and over HTTP as unnamed SSE `data:` frames of AI SDK v5 parts with no `[DONE]` sentinel. On a supervisor with sub-agents, child tokens require opting into `fullStreamEventForwarding.types` and are then available only on `fullStream`, never `textStream`; and combining a supervisor with `outputGuardrails` currently hangs the stream (open issue #1415).

REASONING: I cloned VoltAgent at HEAD and read the implementing source rather than docs. The claimed streaming mechanism checks out at the cited symbols, is MIT-licensed end to end with no cloud tier, and the SSE framing is exactly as described (I read the encoder loop). But two of the claim's own hedges turn out to be structural, not conditional: sub-agent tokens can never reach the parent's `textStream` because the merge is fullStream-only, and even on `fullStream` `text-delta` is excluded by a hard-coded default allow-list. The issue tracker then produced a live defect the claim's guardrail caveat mischaracterises — a supervisor plus output guardrails hangs the SSE stream, unfixed in the newest release with the fix PR still open. Several cited line ranges are also off by enough that the quoted type declaration is not the one at that line. Directionally right, overstated on the multi-agent path, so refuted.

It never builds an OTel `Context` and never sets a remote parent. Real W3C propagation exists in the repo but only on the copilot routes: `contextFromRequestHeaders` (`lib/copilot/request/go/propagation.ts`) is called from `app/api/copilot/chat/stream/route.ts:154` and `lib/copilot/request/otel.ts:233`, and from nowhere on the workflow or chat execute paths. Worse for stitching, workflow spans are created POST HOC: `lib/logs/execution/logging-session.ts:922` calls `createOTelSpansForWorkflowExecution` after the run completes with a backdated `startTime`, and `telemetry.ts:341` parents the root with `context.active()`, which at that point is not the caller's context. So our `turn.voice` span and Sim's `gen_ai.workflow.execute` would land as two roots correlated only by a grep-able trace id in Sim's log lines. Closing it needs a patch to `withRouteHandler` or the execute route, reusing the propagation helper that already exists in the codebase.",
 "prompt_prefix_stability": "Stable \u2014 verified from source, with one caveat. The system prompt comes from the deployed snapshot's `systemPrompt` sub-block and is passed through verbatim (`agent-handler.ts:497`, `addSystemPrompt` at `:1675`); I grepped the agent handler for `new Date()` / `toISOString()` / `Date.now()` and the only hits are latency measurement (`:2390`, `:2495`) and a log timestamp (`:2641`) \u2014 nothing volatile is injected into the prompt, and no run id enters the prefix. Tool definitions keep the order of the block's `tools` array; `prepareToolsWithUsageControl` (`providers/utils.ts:1249`) filters only on each tool's static `usageControl !== 'none'` config, not on per-iteration state, so the tool block does not reorder or shrink between turns. Caching is deliberately engineered rather than accidental: `providers/anthropic/core.ts` builds `system` as a text-block array specifically \"so the shape does not depend on whether prompt caching is on\", puts an `{type:'ephemeral'}` `cache_control` breakpoint on the last system block and on the last tool definition (caching every tool ahead of it), and `providers/anthropic/usage.ts` reports `cache_creation_input_tokens` with the 5m/1h split; `providers/openai/core.ts:107` sets `prompt_cache_key` to `sha256(workflowId:blockId)` \u2014 per-block not per-workflow, with a comment that sharing a key across blocks would lower the hit rate. Caveat: `SIM_AUTO_SYSTEM_PREAMBLE` (`lib/model-router/resolve.ts:80`) is prepended when the block's model is Sim's \"auto\" router. The string itself is static so it does not break caching, but the auto router can pick a different model per run and a model switch is a cold cache. Pin a concrete model on the Agent block.",
 "owns_history": "Sim owns it, in its own Postgres, keyed by an id WE supply \u2014 the arrangement that avoids two drifting histories. `POST /api/chat/{identifier}` accepts only `{input, conversationId, files, password, email}` and forwards `{input, conversationId}` as the workflow input (`app/api/chat/[identifier]/route.ts:212` and `:308`); it does not accept or return a message array, and the route does not load prior turns itself. Persistence is the core **Memory** block (`apps/sim/blocks/blocks/memory.ts`, `apps/sim/tools/memory/{add,get,get_all,delete}.ts`) writing the `memory` pgTable (`packages/db/schema.ts:2734`, unique index on workspace+key). The workflow author wires Memory-get at the top of the graph and Memory-add at the bottom; adding with an existing `conversationId` appends. So history IS re-sent to the model per turn \u2014 read from Postgres and injected into the messages array on every run \u2014 but there is only one copy of it and it is not on our side. For voice we would pass Twilio's CallSid (or a caller-identity key) as `conversationId` and stop keeping a local transcript. Two things to know: growth is unbounded unless the workflow trims it (the same uncapped-memory problem our own scaffold has, one layer over), and because history sits in the messages rather than the prefix it does not disturb prompt-prefix caching. The docs also note intermediate preamble is never persisted \u2014 logs, memory and the block's `content` output carry final-turn text only.",
 "embeddable_in_node": "No. Sim is a Next.js application, not a library: the runtime lives in `apps/sim` and is reachable over HTTP only. The published packages are clients, not engines \u2014 `simstudio-ts-sdk` (`packages/ts-sdk`, v0.2.0, `SimStudioClient({apiKey, baseUrl})` posting to `/api/v2/workflows/{id}/execute`), a Python SDK (`packages/python-sdk`), and the `sim` CLI (`packages/sim-cli`). No package exports the executor, and the executor is not separable in practice: `apps/sim/executor/` reaches Next server modules, Drizzle/Postgres, Redis, the ee tree, and the isolated-vm child-process worker. So the shape is our Node service holding the ConversationRelay WebSocket and calling Sim's SSE endpoint over the network \u2014 one extra container and one extra hop, not an imported runtime. Mitigating: the hop is localhost inside the same compose network, and our existing seam (a public method consuming an async iterable of text) is satisfied by an SSE-to-async-iterable adapter over the `chunk` frames.",
 "visual_editing": "Strong \u2014 the best-scoring axis. A non-engineer edits the Agent block's system prompt in a canvas panel, picks the model and its parameters per block from a large registry (including Ollama for local models), and adds tools from a picker listing both the several-hundred built-in integration blocks and every workspace custom tool; the graph itself is drag-and-wire with condition, router, loop, parallel and sub-workflow blocks. Tool implementations are authored in the same UI (Settings \u2192 Custom Tools: JSON schema tab plus JavaScript tab, with an AI wand that drafts either from a description). Run the draft from the canvas and the terminal panel shows live thinking and tool chips above the block output, because the canvas subscribes to the same agent-events sink the public SSE uses. Deploy, Versions and Promote-to-live are all UI actions. Permissions are graded: view needs Read, create/edit needs Write, delete needs Admin. What a non-engineer cannot do in the UI: diff two prompt versions textually (versions are loaded to the canvas and compared by eye), export to git, or see prompt/completion payloads anywhere except Sim's own Logs view.",
 "maturity": "Very active, very young, single-vendor. 29,648 stars, 3,834 forks, not archived, last push 2026-09-16T16:26Z (today). Release cadence is roughly daily: v0.8.39 published 2026-09-16, v0.8.38 on 2026-09-15, v0.8.37 also 2026-09-15. 873 commits in the last 30 days on the default branch. Still pre-1.0 on a 0.8.x line, and the SDK README documents that 0.2.0 was a breaking release shipped deliberately as a minor bump so `^0.1.2` would not pick it up \u2014 expect churn in exactly the surfaces we would integrate against. Images are published by the vendor on GitHub Container Registry (`ghcr.io/simstudioai/{simstudio,realtime,migrations,cron}`), tracking `latest` unless pinned via `SIM_VERSION`; the docs tell you to pin for production. Copyright holder is Sim Studio, Inc., with a commercial cloud at sim.ai and `sales@sim.ai` for the enterprise tier \u2014 the open core is a funnel, and the `ee/` boundary is where that shows. The repo is a bun + turbo monorepo: 21,313 files, `apps/{sim,docs,realtime,pii,desktop}` and 24 workspace packages. Docs are extensive and unusually candid about their own traps (e.g. that `telemetry.enabled: true` on Helm receives nothing until you also clear `NEXT_TELEMETRY_DISABLED`).",
 "fit_verdict": "Passes all three hard requirements \u2014 the only candidate so far that does \u2014 but two of the passes carry conditions that need a decision before adoption, and it does not retire Langfuse.\n\nHard requirement 1, self-hostable OSS in a local compose stack: PASS. `docker-compose.prod.yml`, six ghcr images, no control plane, no licence key anywhere in code. The caveat is legal, not technical: `apps/sim/ee/` is proprietary and `apps/sim/tools/index.ts:82` imports it statically, so booting the app and running any tool executes Enterprise-licensed code whose grant covers \"development, testing, and evaluation\" and \"internal non-production use\" only. A five-user month-long demo reads as inside that grant; a production deployment does not. Nothing we need sits behind a paid feature gate \u2014 round one's custom-blocks worry was a name collision, and the two things it was really selling (per-block model selection, in-builder tools) are both core.\n\nHard requirement 2, token-level streaming during tool-calling turns: PASS, and this produced the sharpest finding in the report. Set `X-Sim-Stream-Protocol: agent-events-v1` and `chunk` frames arrive per model delta, including mid-turn while tools are in play. But those deltas are speculative: a turn that resolves to tool calls is retracted with `chunk_reset`, and ConversationRelay cannot un-speak audio. Either accept occasional double-speak on tool turns, or drop the header and lose token cadence. Decide that before building \u2014 it is the difference between a barge-in-quality voice agent and a one-utterance-per-turn one.\n\nHard requirement 3, tool definitions and implementations authored in the builder: PASS, cleanly and better than expected. JSON schema plus a JavaScript body, both typed into the UI, executed in an out-of-process isolated-vm isolate with brokered SSRF-validated fetch and `{{SECRET}}` binding. Nothing forwards to code we host.\n\nWhere it earns its place beyond the filters: versioning. A deployment snapshot freezes the prompt, the model, the parameters and the tool selection as one numbered version with instant Promote-to-live rollback, which fixes the split we live with today (prompt in Langfuse, tools in TypeScript, versioned independently and able to disagree).\n\nWhere it costs us: OTLP carries `gen_ai.*` model/token/cost spans but explicitly no prompts, completions, tool arguments or results, so Langfuse stays for payloads; the executor's own `function.execute` / `router.evaluate` spans are dropped by an allowlist before export; `traceparent` is read for log grep only and never as a remote parent, so a voice trace and a Sim trace are two roots today; and the runtime is not embeddable, so it is an extra container and an extra hop rather than an imported library.\n\nRecommendation: strongest candidate, adopt-with-conditions rather than adopt. Three open engineering items \u2014 the `chunk_reset` voice policy, a small patch to parent workflow spans on an incoming `traceparent`, and an SSE-to-async-iterable adapter for the voice channel's send method. One non-engineering item: a licence decision about the ee tree.",

### verdict refuted=True
CLAIM: Sim is licensed Apache-2.0 (root) + LicenseRef-Sim-Enterprise (apps/sim/ee/), self-hosted commercial internal use of the needed capabilities is permitted with nothing behind a paid tier; ee/ contains exactly 13 named dirs; none of the three hard requirements is gated; ENTERPRISE_ENABLED=1 + NEXT_PUBLIC_ENTERPRISE_ENABLED=1 flips the whole suite on with no licence-key check anywhere.

CORRECTED: Refuted as overstated in five specifics; the licence structure and the "not excisable" finding are correct as stated.

VERIFIED (primary source, simstudioai/sim @ main, pushed 2026-09-16):
- Root `/LICENSE` is verbatim Apache-2.0, appendix line 190 reads "Copyright 2026 Sim Studio, Inc."; `/NOTICE` exists ("Copyright 2026 Sim Studio"). GitHub's licence API reports `spdx_id: Apache-2.0` for the repo as a whole.
- `apps/sim/ee/LICENSE` is a separate proprietary "Sim Enterprise License", "Copyright (c) 2025-present Sim Studio, Inc." Grant is verbatim as quoted ("Development, testing, and evaluation purposes", "Internal non-production use", "Production use of the Software requires a valid Sim Enterprise subscription"), and the four restrictions are quoted verbatim and correctly.
- The ee tree is genuinely not excisable, and `apps/sim/ee/README.md` says so in the vendor's own words: "Enterprise features are imported directly throughout the codebase. The `ee/` directory is required at build time." All four cited import sites are exact at the cited lines: `apps/sim/tools/index.ts:82`, `apps/sim/app/layout.tsx:9`, `apps/sim/app/manifest.ts:3`, `apps/sim/background/fork-content-copy.ts:5`; `chat.tsx:24` is `import SSOAuth from '@/ee/sso/components/sso-auth'`.
- The `EnterpriseFeature` union in `apps/sim/lib/core/config/enterprise-entitlements.ts:28-42` is exactly the 14 members listed. `ENTERPRISE_FEATURE_LEGACY_DEFAULTS` has `sandboxes: false` (line 85) with the comment justifying it; `inbox` and `sessionPolicies` and `whitelabeling` are the only `true` entries.
- `assertPermissionsAllowed` is at `permission-check.ts:560` exactly.
- Per-block model selection is the core Agent block's `model` sub-block at `apps/sim/blocks/blocks/agent.ts:144` (a `combobox`, `defaultValue: 'claude-sonnet-5'`) — core, not gated.
- The name collision is real: `apps/sim/ee/custom-blocks/components/custom-blocks.tsx:94` carries the exact string "Custom blocks require an Enterprise plan. Contact your admin to enable them."
- No licence-key enforcement: GitHub code search for `ENTERPRISE_LICENSE` and `licenseKey` in the repo returns 0 hits. Gating is env-var only.

WHERE IT IS OVERSTATED:
1. "nothing behind a paid tier" is contradicted by the claim's own evidence and is wrong as a headline. The ee LICENSE bars production use without a subscription AND bars modification/derivative works/redistribution of the ee code — rights the Apache-2.0 root badge would otherwise grant. Since ee code executes at boot (`layout.tsx`, `manifest.ts`) and on every tool call (`tools/index.ts`), the deployed artifact as a whole is not usable under Apache-2.0 terms. Correct statement: the *capabilities we need* are in Apache-2.0 code, but *the running application* is mixed-licence, and its non-production/evaluation grant is a terms judgement about our deployment, not an OSI permission.
2. "`apps/sim/ee/` contains exactly" those 13 dirs is wrong. The contents API returns 14 directories — the list plus `components/` — alongside `LICENSE` and `README.md`.
3. "flip the whole suite on with ENTERPRISE_ENABLED=1 plus NEXT_PUBLIC_ENTERPRISE_ENABLED=1" is not true of the whole suite. `.env.example:196-199` enumerates what the master switch covers: "organizations, SSO, permission groups, audit logs, custom blocks, whitelabeling, session policies, data retention, data drains, forks, and the inbox" — sandboxes, scim and usageMonitoring are absent. `resolveSandboxFeatureAvailability` (same file, end) returns `remoteProviderEnabled && (billingEnabled || deploymentEntitled)` with the docstring "Neither dimension may substitute for the other", and sandboxes have their own `SANDBOXES_ENABLED` / `NEXT_PUBLIC_SANDBOXES_ENABLED` / `SANDBOX_PROVIDER` block at `.env.example:31-49`. Additionally `.env.example:220-222`: "Most enterprise features read their settings from the organization that owns a workspace, so a deployment needs an organization for them to apply" — so `INSTANCE_ORG_NAME` (or Admin-API org creation) is a further prerequisite the two flags do not satisfy.
4. "`assertPermissionsAllowed` … short-circuits on a permissive config" understates it. The early return at the top fires only when `blockType` is access-control-exempt AND no model/toolKind/toolId is present; otherwise, with no `userId`/`workspaceId` it still calls `mergeEnvAllowlist(null)` and can throw `ToolNotAllowedError` or the model/block-type errors from an env allowlist. Enterprise-licensed code doesn't just run — it can deny a tool call on a self-host with `accessControl` false.
5. Path error: `custom-tool-code-field.tsx` is at `apps/sim/app/workspace/[workspaceId]/components/custom-tool-editor/custom-tool-code-field.tsx`, not under `settings/components/`. The settings dir `custom-tools/` exists but its subtree is `components/custom-tool-detail/custom-tool-detail.tsx`, with no `custom-tool-editor`.

No open issues contradict the gating story; searches for "enterprise" and "license" in the tracker surface only unrelated feature requests and Docker/Ollama bugs.

REASONING: I fetched the two LICENSE files, NOTICE, ee/README.md, .env.example, enterprise-entitlements.ts, permission-check.ts, agent.ts, the five cited import sites, and the ee/custom-blocks component, plus the GitHub contents/licence APIs and code search. The licence quotations, line numbers and the "ee is not excisable" conclusion all hold exactly — this is the most accurate of the licence claims I've checked. But three factual overstatements survive verification: ee/ has a 14th directory (`components/`), the master switch demonstrably does not cover sandboxes/scim/usageMonitoring and additionally needs an instance organization, and the description of assertPermissionsAllowed's short-circuit is looser than the code. The headline "nothing behind a paid tier" also cannot stand next to the claim's own restriction list: ee code runs at boot and per tool call under a licence that forbids production use and forbids modification, which is a materially different posture from an Apache-2.0 self-host. Per instructions, overstated counts as refuted.

### verdict refuted=True
CLAIM: Sim allows tool definitions AND their implementations to be authored inside the builder rather than in our application source. Mechanism offered: Yes — genuinely authored in the UI, both halves, and core rather than enterprise. Settings → Custom Tools → Add gives two tabs: Schema, a JSON blob in OpenAI function-calling format, and Code, a JavaScript function body where schema parameters arrive as bare variables. Secrets interpolate as {{KEY}} from Settings → Secrets, bound at execution time and masked in the trace copy. await and fetch() are available plus Node built-ins (crypto, Buffer); no npm packages. Saved tools appear in any Agent block's Add Tools picker across the workspace. Executor routes to executeInIsolatedVM by default, or executeInSandbox only when the enterprise-gated `sandboxes` entitlement AND a remote provider are both configured. One restriction: custom tools work only inside Agent blocks.

CORRECTED: The core capability holds: on a plain Apache-2.0 self-host you author both halves of a tool in the UI — a JSON schema in OpenAI function-calling format and a JavaScript body — persisted per workspace and offered in any Agent block's tool picker, with `{{KEY}}` secrets bound at execution time rather than pasted into source. `isCustomTool` param destructuring in `execute-request.ts` confirms bare variables (`const city = params.city;` is prepended). Three parts of the claim are wrong.

(1) There are NO Node built-ins. The runtime that actually executes custom tools is `executeInIsolatedVM`, and the repo pins its global surface in `apps/sim/lib/execution/sim-helpers.smoke.test.ts` ("pins which globals the fast local runtime actually provides"): `sim: true, fetch: true, console: true, JSON: true, Uint8Array: true` and explicitly `Buffer: false, require: false, process: false, atob: false, TextDecoder: false, crypto: false, setTimeout: false`. The comment says so in words: "plain ECMAScript plus `fetch` and `sim.*` — no Node built-ins, and notably no `crypto`, `TextDecoder`, or even `setTimeout`." The docs page `apps/docs/content/docs/agents/custom-tools.mdx` claims "Node.js built-ins — Access to `crypto`, `Buffer`, and other standard modules"; the source contradicts its own docs. No `crypto` in `isolated-vm-worker.cjs` either. Not even `setTimeout` — so a custom tool cannot sleep, retry with backoff, hash, or base64 without hand-rolling it in pure JS.

(2) The routing description is wrong for custom tools specifically, and the escape hatch is closed. `useRemoteSandbox` in `apps/sim/lib/function-execution/execute-request.ts` is `usesMothershipSandbox || (remoteSandboxEnabled && !isCustomTool && (…))` — the `!isCustomTool` term means a custom tool is hardcoded to the local isolated-vm path even on a fully entitled cloud/enterprise deployment with E2B or Daytona configured. Only Sim's internal `auth.sandboxProfile === 'mothership'` bypasses it. For ordinary blocks, "add an import to get the remote sandbox" is the documented escape from the bare-ECMAScript runtime; for a custom tool that route does not exist, and a custom tool with an import slips past the `hasImports && !remoteSandboxEnabled` guard when a provider IS configured only to land in isolated-vm and fail there. The `sandboxes` enterprise entitlement (`apps/sim/lib/core/config/enterprise-entitlements.ts`, `isSandboxDeploymentEntitled` in `lib/core/config/env-flags.ts`) exists but is irrelevant to custom tools.

(3) Settings → Custom Tools has no tabs. `settings/components/custom-tools/components/custom-tool-detail/custom-tool-detail.tsx` documents itself as "the Schema and Code editors stacked (no tabs — the page has room for both)". The two-tab Schema/Code surface is the canvas modal reached from an Agent block's tool picker (`…/sub-block/components/tool-input/components/custom-tool-modal/custom-tool-modal.tsx`, `ToolSection = 'schema' | 'code'`). The shared field components live at `apps/sim/app/workspace/[workspaceId]/components/custom-tool-editor/`, not under the settings directory.

Practical consequence: for anything beyond `fetch` + plain JS — signing a request, timing out, encoding a payload — you are back to writing code in your own application and calling it over HTTP from the custom tool.

REASONING: Read the primary sources in a shallow clone of simstudioai/sim (commit c0f4717). LICENSE is Apache 2.0, Copyright 2026 Sim Studio, Inc. — the licence permits self-hosting the feature, and custom tools are not gated by `enterprise-entitlements.ts`. The docs page matches the claim almost verbatim, which is why the claim reads well; but the docs are contradicted by the executor source and by the repo's own pinning test. The decisive artifacts are the `!isCustomTool` term in `useRemoteSandbox` (custom tools can never reach the remote sandbox) and the globals-pinning assertion in `sim-helpers.smoke.test.ts`, which drives the real `executeInIsolatedVM` and asserts `crypto: false, Buffer: false`. Issue-tracker search turned up only stale closed reports (#260, #284, #32 from 2025), so the refutation rests on source, not on user complaints. The Agent-block-only restriction and the workspace-wide picker are as stated; the `{{KEY}}` secret binding and trace masking are corroborated by `resolvedSecretNames` / `appendResolvedSecretNames` in `execute-request.ts` and `RESOLVED_SECRET_NAMES_FIELD` in `lib/execution/private-tool-metadata.ts`. Overstated on the runtime surface, the executor gating, and the UI shape — therefore refuted.

### verdict refuted=True
CLAIM: Sim streams token-level model output incrementally during an agent turn that includes tool calls, via two endpoints (deployed chat `POST /api/chat/{identifier}` and authenticated `POST /api/v2/workflows/{id}/execute`), both requiring `X-Sim-Stream-Protocol: agent-events-v1` for live token cadence; transport is plain SSE (`data: <json>\n`, `encodeSSE`) with 15 s keepalive comments; frames are the `ChatStreamFrame` union (`{blockId,chunk}` with absence of `event` as discriminator, `chunk_reset`, `thinking`, `tool`, `final`, `error`, `stream_error`, then literal `data: "[DONE]"`); a standard SSE client parses it, an OpenAI client does not, and ~30 lines of custom splitter adapts it to a voice channel.

CORRECTED: Substantially correct and unusually well-sourced — the central capability survives every refutation test — but overstated in four specific places, so it should not be repeated verbatim.

WHAT HOLDS (verified at simstudioai/sim SHA c0f4717, which is also release tag v0.8.39, published 2026-09-16):
- Licence permits it. `LICENSE` is Apache License 2.0, "Copyright 2026 Sim Studio, Inc." No cloud/enterprise gate on this path: `createStreamingResponse` in `apps/sim/lib/workflows/streaming/streaming.ts` has no `isHosted`/`isProd` branch.
- It is a real agent loop with tools, not a plain model node. `apps/sim/providers/anthropic/core.ts:445` selects the live loop on `if (request.stream && anthropicTools && anthropicTools.length > 0)` and returns `streamFormat: 'agent-events-v1'`. Nothing gates it on the event-policy flags.
- `apps/sim/providers/streaming-tool-loop-shared.ts` `STREAMING_TOOL_CALL_PROVIDERS` is exactly the eight named: openai, anthropic, azure-anthropic, groq, deepseek, google, vertex, bedrock.
- `apps/sim/providers/stream-events.ts` defines `AgentStreamEvent` with `text_delta`/`turn_end`/`thinking_delta`/`tool_call_start`/`tool_call_end` and `TextDeltaClassification = 'intermediate' | 'final' | 'pending'`.
- `apps/sim/providers/stream-pump.ts` `handleEvent` is quoted correctly: `await dispatchToSinks(event)` runs on every `text_delta` before the `turn === 'pending'` test, while `pendingTurnText += event.text` buffers for the legacy byte stream and is flushed only under `if (buffered && event.turn === 'final')`.
- `streaming.ts:675-691` matches the cited line range exactly, including `if (clientAcceptsProtocol && streamingExec.subscribe)`, the per-`text_delta` `emitAnswerChunk`, and the `chunk_reset` enqueue on an intermediate `turn_end`.
- Header alone buys live cadence — no policy flag needed. `sinkAnswerText = clientAcceptsProtocol && Boolean(streamingExec.subscribe) && streamingExec.clientStreamTransformed !== true` (`streaming.ts:654-657`); `emitThinking`/`emitToolCalls` are separate.
- Keepalive is 15 s: `const STREAM_KEEPALIVE_INTERVAL_MS = 15_000` (`streaming.ts:52`), emitted via `encodeSSEComment('keepalive')`.
- Deployed-chat body: `const { input, password, email, conversationId, files } = parsedBody` and `const workflowInput: any = { input, conversationId }` (chat route lines 212, 308).
- The 400 is real: `if (hasAgentStreamOptions && !clientAcceptsAgentStreamProtocol(req.headers))` returns BAD_REQUEST (v2 execute route :422-425).
- The voice caveat is accurate and matches the source's own comments — `chunk_reset` means discard, and speculative text on a tool-calling turn is inherent.
- Evidence is a spec doc plus source, not marketing.

WHERE IT IS OVERSTATED:
1. Transport framing is wrong by a newline. `encodeSSE` in `apps/sim/lib/core/utils/sse.ts` returns `` `data: ${JSON.stringify(data)}\n\n` `` — a blank-line-terminated SSE event, not `data: <json>\n`. (Harmless in practice: `readSSELines` and the docs' reference client both split on single `\n` and ignore blank lines.)
2. The frame union is enumerated incompletely, and the negotiation vocabulary is larger than one token. `ChatStreamFrame` also includes `ChatStreamOutputFrame` `{blockId, event:'output', data}`, gated by a SECOND protocol token the claim never mentions: `CHAT_OUTPUT_PROTOCOL_V1 = 'chat-outputs-v1'` via `clientAcceptsChatOutputProtocol`, deployed-chat only (`streaming.ts:519-522`). A client that assumes `agent-events-v1` is the whole protocol will meet an undocumented-to-it frame type.
3. "Without it you get the whole settled final turn in one frame" is true only of turns that run through a streaming tool loop. For a tool-free agent block, a `text` -format provider, or any provider outside `STREAMING_TOOL_CALL_PROVIDERS`, deltas carry `turn: 'final'` or `undefined`, `isImmediateFinalText` sends them straight to `enqueueAnswerText`, and `streaming.ts:718-720` (`if (!sinkAnswerText && !deferSelectedOutputs) emitAnswerChunk(textChunk)`) emits a `chunk` frame per byte chunk. A header-less client therefore already gets token-by-token text there; the single-frame collapse is specific to the tool-calling case.
4. The workflow-API preconditions are understated. Beyond the header 400 the claim cites, `stream: true` is independently mandatory and produces two further 400s: `selectedOutputs requires stream: true` (:409-412) and `includeThinking and includeToolCalls require stream: true` (:420). Also the route is `/api/v2/workflows/[workflowId]/execute`; `{id}` is the docs' shorthand.

Minor: no open issue was found reporting the feature failing as documented — but the protocol landed very recently (it is in v0.8.39, released the same day), so the absence of bug reports is weak evidence of field-proven behaviour rather than strong evidence of correctness. And the claim's own concession is right: adapting this to a voice channel does require writing client code, roughly the size of the docs' reference client (~45 lines, not 30).

REASONING: I went to the repository rather than any marketing surface, and every file path the claim cited exists with the content asserted — including the `streaming.ts:675-691` line range, which is rare in a claim of this length. The hard refutation triggers all failed: Apache 2.0 licence with no tier gate, the live path is selected on `request.stream` plus tools present rather than on any event-policy flag (so it genuinely streams through an agent loop with tool calls, not just a plain model node), frames are ordinary JSON over ordinary SSE that `readSSELines` and the shipped reference client both parse, and the evidence offered is source plus a specification `.mdx`. The feature is in a tagged release, not main-only.

So I could not refute the capability. Applying the brief's "overstated counts as refuted" rule, I set refuted=true on four concrete inaccuracies: `encodeSSE` emits `\n\n` not `\n`; the `ChatStreamFrame` union includes an `output` frame gated by an entirely separate and unmentioned `chat-outputs-v1` protocol token; the "one frame without the header" statement holds only for tool-loop turns because `isImmediateFinalText` plus `streaming.ts:718-720` already give header-less clients per-chunk frames otherwise; and the workflow API enforces `stream: true` with two additional 400s the claim omitted. I also flagged that the release is one day old, which makes the empty issue tracker uninformative either way.

 "streaming_transport": "`POST /v3/chat` (public, PAT-authenticated; `stream` defaults to true \u2014 the sync path is only taken when `req.Stream != nil && !*req.Stream`). Event names come from backend/domain/conversation/agentrun/entity/const.go \u2014 the one that matters is `conversation.message.delta`, alongside `conversation.chat.created`/`in_progress`/`completed`/`failed`, `conversation.message.completed`, `conversation.error`, `conversation.stream.done`. The `data` payload for a delta is a `ChatV3MessageDetail` whose `content` field holds ONLY the new fragment (see below), plus id/conversation_id/chat_id/role/type/content_type/reasoning_content. A standard SSE client (EventSource, `sseclient`, `eventsource-parser`) parses these frames as-is. An OpenAI client CANNOT: there is no `chat.completion.chunk` object, no `choices[].delta`, and the terminator is `event: conversation.stream.done` rather than `data: [DONE]`. Adapting to our voice channel's async-iterable-of-text seam is a ~20-line parser: subscribe, filter `event === 'conversation.message.delta'`, ignore frames where `reasoning_content` is set, yield `JSON.parse(data).content`.",
 "streaming_evidence": "Backend source, not marketing. Chain: (1) backend/api/handler/coze/agent_run_service.go `ChatV3` sets `X-Accel-Buffering: no`, wraps the request in `sseImpl.NewSSESender(sse.NewStream(c))` and hands the sender to the application layer BEFORE the turn runs. (2) backend/infra/sse/impl/sse/*.go `Send` is a one-line passthrough to `Stream.Publish`. (3) hertz-contrib/sse v0.1.0 sse.go:104 `Publish` = `Encode(c.w, event)` then `return c.w.Flush()` \u2014 an explicit flush per event, so nothing accumulates in the writer. (4) backend/application/conversation/openapi_agent_run.go `pullStream` is a bare `for { chunk, err := streamer.Recv(); ... sseSender.Send(...) }` loop with no batching, no coalescing timer, no buffer. (5) The producer, backend/domain/conversation/agentrun/internal/singleagent_run.go, case `message.MessageTypeAnswer`: `streamMsg, receErr := chunk.ModelAnswer.Recv()` \u2026 `sendAnswerMsg.Content = streamMsg.Content` \u2026 `art.MessageEvent.SendMsgEvent(entity.RunEventMessageDelta, sendAnswerMsg, art.SW)`. Note `= streamMsg.Content`, not `= fullContent.String()` \u2014 the accumulating `fullContent` buffer is written only to the terminal `handlerAnswer` persistence call at EOF. That is the exact line that distinguishes a real token stream from a growing-snapshot stream, and it is the incremental form. Nginx is configured for it too: `proxy_read_timeout 600s; # 600s for SSE` in docker/nginx/conf.d/default.conf:32.",
 "streaming_caveats": "Streaming survives a tool-calling turn, but with three specifics worth knowing. (1) The tool-call round trip itself is silent: in the `MessageTypeAnswer` loop, when `len(streamMsg.ToolCalls) > 0` the code sets `isToolCalls = true` and, at EOF, `if isToolCalls { break }` \u2014 it skips the finalise path and emits no text for that segment, because the model produced arguments rather than prose. Deltas resume on the post-tool model call. So during a tool turn the caller hears a gap of exactly the tool's own latency; nothing is buffered and released in a burst, but nothing is spoken either. (2) Filler text WHILE a tool runs does stream: `MessageTypeToolMidAnswer` pumps `chunk.ToolMidAnswer.Recv()` and emits a delta per chunk, tagging `Ext[\"message_title\"]` from `Extra[\"workflow_node_name\"]` \u2014 that is how a workflow-as-tool's intermediate output reaches the wire mid-turn. Same for `MessageTypeToolAsAnswer`. (3) The chatflow path is opt-in per node, and this is the trap. Both backend/domain/workflow/internal/nodes/exit/exit.go and .../emitter/emitter.go read `n.Data.Inputs.StreamingOutput` from the saved canvas and set `StreamConfigs.RequireStreamingInput` to true or FALSE accordingly; with it false the node exposes `Invoke` (whole value at once) instead of `Transform(ctx, *schema.StreamReader[...])`. A flow author who leaves the Output/End node's streaming toggle off gets the whole answer in one event, and nothing in the API surfaces that a node is configured that way. Prefer the single-agent `/v3/chat` path, where streaming is unconditional, over `/v1/workflows/chat`. Also: `reasoning_content` arrives as `conversation.message.delta` frames on the same stream, so a naive consumer would synthesise the model's chain-of-thought to the caller \u2014 filter it.  "prompt_prefix_stability": "NOT byte-stable \u2014 it is invalidated on every single turn, by design, and I can point at the line. The ReAct system prompt template is `REACT_SYSTEM_PROMPT_JINJA2` in backend/domain/agent/singleagent/internal/agentflow/system_prompt.go, and its THIRD line \u2014 before the safety guidelines, before the persona, before anything cacheable \u2014 is `It is {{ time }} now.`. That placeholder is filled at backend/domain/agent/singleagent/internal/agentflow/node_prompt_variables.go:42 with `variables[placeholderOfTime] = time.Now().Format(\"Monday 2006/01/02 15:04:05 -07\")`. Second resolution, rendered per run, positioned in the prefix. Provider prompt caching matches on a byte-identical prefix, so a timestamp that changes every second at character ~60 means the cache never hits: every turn of every conversation is a cold turn. For us that is not a rounding error. Our own measurement is that the framework costs 84 ms and everything else is model time-to-first-chunk, that a tool-free turn is 586\u2013801 ms warm, and that the memory block being absorbed by the prompt cache on turns 2+ is precisely why turns after the first are fast. Coze Studio's template removes that mechanism. It gets worse further down the same template, which also inlines `{{ memory_variables }}` and `{{ knowledge }}` \u2014 the retrieved knowledge chunks, which vary per query \u2014 into the SYSTEM message rather than appending them as a user-turn or tool-result block, so even with the timestamp patched out the prefix would still churn whenever retrieval returns something different. Fixing it means editing Go source and rebuilding the image (the template is a compile-time `const`, not configuration), which forks the project. This is the single most consequential finding for a voice use case, because the first turn is the turn a caller judges.",
 "owns_history": "Coze owns it, server-side, and it is re-assembled per turn from its own store \u2014 so adopting it creates the two-histories-drifting failure mode directly. Conversation state lives in the `conversation` and `message` MySQL tables keyed by Coze's own `conversation_id`. On each run, `AgentRuntime` calls `getHistory` (backend/domain/conversation/agentrun/internal/run.go:177), which resolves recent run IDs and fetches messages via `crossmessage.DefaultSVC().GetByRunIDs(ctx, conversationID, runIDS)`, then `SetHistoryMsg`. `getAgentHistoryRounds` (internal/agent_info.go:29) caps the window from `ModelInfo.ShortMemoryPolicy.HistoryRound`, defaulting to `ConversationTurnsDefault = 100` turns. `historyPairs` (internal/message_builder.go:437) drops unpaired messages so the model sees complete question/answer pairs, and `transMessageToSchemaMessage` converts them for the model call. `ChatV3Request` carries `additional_messages` for the CURRENT turn only, and there is no `auto_save_history` flag in the struct \u2014 so you cannot ask Coze to be stateless and drive it from our own transcript. Practically: TAC's memory/Recall would keep building the caller's history on our side while Coze independently builds its own from the same turns, the two would diverge the moment a turn is barged into, cancelled (`/v3/chat/cancel`), or replayed, and the model would be reading Coze's copy. Interruption is a live concern for ConversationRelay specifically \u2014 our interrupt handler parks the turn boundary, and a barged-in turn that we truncate locally is still whatever Coze persisted server-side. Managing that means one side becomes advisory, and it would have to be ours.",
 "embeddable_in_node": "No. There is no in-process option, and no package to name. The runtime is a compiled Go monolith: `backend/` builds a single binary invoked as `command: ['/app/opencoze']`, on the CloudWeGo Hertz HTTP server with the CloudWeGo Eino framework doing the agent graph (`github.com/cloudwego/eino/schema` StreamReader is the internal stream type). The published artefacts are Docker images (`cozedev/coze-studio-server`, `cozedev/coze-studio-web`), not libraries. `frontend/` is a Rush monorepo of React/TypeScript UI packages \u2014 those are builder UI components, not the orchestrator, and `rush.json` publishes nothing runnable as an agent runtime. So the only integration is network-boundary: our Node service holds a PAT and calls `POST /v3/chat` over HTTP, consuming the SSE body. For our seam that is workable \u2014 the voice channel's public method takes an async iterable of text, and an SSE-to-async-iterable adapter over `conversation.message.delta` frames is short \u2014 but it adds a network hop plus 11 containers inside the request path for every caller utterance, against today's direct in-process model call. Note also the WebSocket asymmetry: ConversationRelay speaks WebSocket to us, and Coze offers only HTTP+SSE, so each turn opens a fresh POST rather than reusing a persistent connection.",
 "visual_editing": "Strongest in the field, and the round-one superlative is earned once you say what it is a superlative AT. A non-engineer can absolutely edit a prompt: the agent builder has a persona/prompt pane, a model picker with a short-memory-rounds control, onboarding copy, suggested replies, shortcut commands, variables, knowledge binding, and a debug chat beside it \u2014 all backed by the `single_agent_draft`/`single_agent_version` columns, so what they edit is what ships. Wiring a tool is a two-tier experience, and the tiers differ in difficulty. Binding an EXISTING plugin or workflow to an agent is genuinely non-engineer work: pick from a list, done. AUTHORING a new tool is not: for a plugin you fill in an OpenAPI operation schema, a sub-URL, an HTTP method and auth \u2014 that is an engineer's task wearing a form's clothing, and the implementation still lives on a server someone else runs. Authoring real logic means building a workflow on the canvas (Code, Plugin, LLM, Knowledge Retriever, Selector, Loop, Batch, Variable Aggregator, Intent Detector, Question, Sub-workflow, HTTP Requester, Database, JSON, Text Processor, Emitter, Exit) and writing Python in the Code node \u2014 visual scaffolding around a code editor. There is per-node debugging (`/nodeDebug`, `WorkflowNodeDebugV2`), tree validation (`/validate_tree`), test-run and test-resume, and a node search/template palette, which is real craft and better tooling than anything we would build. Two frictions worth naming: node metadata ships Chinese `Name`/`Desc` alongside `EnUSName`/`EnUSDescription`, so English is a translation layer and some surfaces show through; and the trace/observability panel is wired to stub endpoints, so a builder debugging a live conversation gets an empty view. Scored, not a filter: this is the best visual authoring I verified, and it is also the thing that makes the missing tracing and the timestamped prompt prefix easy to overlook.",
 "maturity": "Large audience, stalled engineering \u2014 and the stall is recent enough that a round-one sweep would have missed it. 21,598 stars, 3,111 forks, 571 open issues, created 2025-06-26, published by ByteDance under the coze-dev org (releases signed off by fanlv@bytedance.com; images under the `cozedev` Docker Hub namespace). Latest release is v0.5.1, published 2026-02-05 \u2014 SEVEN months before today. Cadence via the commits API since 2026-04-01: FOUR commits total, at 2026-04-09, 2026-04-09, 2026-04-20, and 2026-07-29, the last being `fix(infra): align statefulset servicename with actual service name (#2723)`. `pushed_at` is 2026-07-29, so the default branch has been quiet for about seven weeks and has seen four commits in five and a half months, three of them fixes (two OAuth phishing/CSRF, one nested sub-workflow path corruption). Half a year of releases behind, 571 open issues, and CVE-2026-7023 reported against \u22640.5.1 with the vendor recorded as non-responsive, is the profile of a code drop whose maintenance moved back inside ByteDance rather than an actively co-developed project \u2014 consistent with README's framing, \"derived from the Coze Development Platform... we have made its core engine completely open\". The 21.6k stars measure the SaaS's reputation, not this repo's velocity. For a five-user month-long demo the velocity barely matters; for the SQL-injection CVE with no patch it does, and it argues for keeping the stack off any public host regardless.",
 "fit_verdict": "Passes all three hard requirements and I would still not adopt it. Requirement 1, self-hostable OSS with no control plane and no paid gate: clean pass, verbatim Apache-2.0, 11 local containers, zero enterprise gating in the code. Requirement 2, token-level streaming: TRUE, and proven from the backend rather than inferred \u2014 `sendAnswerMsg.Content = streamMsg.Content` per model chunk, a flush per SSE event, and deltas that keep flowing through a tool-calling turn. Requirement 3, tool implementations in the builder: pass via the workflow Code node's sandboxed Python, though NOT via plugins, which are definition-only HTTP forwards to code we would still host. So round one's \"survivor with the strongest visual authoring\" holds up on the filters, and the authoring really is the best I verified. What sinks it is the two things round one never asked. First, the system prompt's third line is `It is {{ time }} now.` filled from `time.Now()` at second resolution \u2014 provider prompt caching is defeated on every turn, permanently, in a compile-time `const` we cannot configure away. Our own numbers say the framework costs 84 ms and the rest is model time-to-first-chunk, and that warm turns are 586\u2013801 ms; this makes every turn a cold turn, on the exact metric a caller judges. Second, tracing does not exist in the open-source build \u2014 the trace endpoints are stubs returning `{}`, otel is `// indirect` only, no traceparent is accepted, so Coze cannot retire Langfuse AND takes the agent's internals out of our Langfuse traces at the same time. Net: we would trade one visible, instrumented, cache-warm hand-written agent for a black box with worse first-token latency, plus 11 containers, a second conversation history that will drift against TAC's, no version pinning on the public chat API, no DSL export, and an unpatched SQL-injection CVE. Worth prototyping to see the builder \u2014 one afternoon, `pnpm`-free, images pull clean \u2014 but not worth migrating to. If any part of Coze is worth adopting it is Coze Loop, separately, for the tracing question, which the sweep should evaluate on its own terms.",
 "disqualifiers": [
  "Not a hard-requirement failure, but the decisive one: the system prompt template hard-codes `It is {{ time }} now.` on line 3, filled per run from `time.Now().Format(\"Monday 2006/01/02 15:04:05 -07\")`, so the cacheable prefix changes every second and provider prompt caching NEVER hits. Every turn is a cold turn. It is a compile-time const in Go \u2014 unfixable without forking and rebuilding the image.",
  "No tracing whatsoever in the open-source build. `ListRootSpans` and `GetTraceSDK` bind their request and return an empty struct with HTTP 200; all OpenTelemetry modules in go.mod are `// indirect`. It cannot replace Langfuse, and because it emits no spans it also removes the agent's internals from the Langfuse traces we keep.",
  "Does not accept a W3C traceparent anywhere (zero occurrences of the string in the repo), so no incoming trace can be continued into the orchestration.",
  "Coze owns conversation history in its own MySQL tables keyed by its own conversation_id and re-assembles it per turn (default 100 rounds). There is no auto_save_history flag on ChatV3Request, so it cannot be driven statelessly from our transcript \u2014 two histories will coexist and drift on barge-in, cancel, or replay.",
  "`ChatV3Request` has no version field, so the public chat API cannot pin an agent version \u2014 every call gets the most recently published one. Rollback means re-publishing (append-only history), and there is no diff. Prompt and tool-binding writes exist only on internal session-authed `/api/...` routes, nothing under /v1 or /v3.",
  "No flow export. The canvas is a JSON blob in `workflow_draft.canvas`/`workflow_version`; git-tracking it means scraping the undocumented internal `POST /api/workflow_api/canvas` and pushing back via `/save`. There is no DSL, no YAML, no import/export endpoint.",
  "Operational weight: 11 containers (Milvus + etcd + MinIO + a 3-process NSQ cluster + Elasticsearch + MySQL + Redis) in the request path, versus our current 3, for a five-user demo. The mysql entrypoint also curls https://atlasgo.sh at container start to apply the schema.",
  "CVE-2026-7023, SQL injection in the databaseTool's `ExecuteSQL`, affects \u22640.5.1 with a public PoC and the vendor recorded as non-responsive; the latest release IS 0.5.1 (2026-02-05). HEAD adds `validateCustomSQL`/`validateParsedSQL` guards but I did not verify they close it.",
  "Engineering has largely stopped: four commits since 2026-04-01, last push 2026-07-29, latest release seven months old, 571 open issues.",
  "Setting `CODE_RUNNER_TYPE=local` swaps the Deno/Pyodide sandbox for bare `python3` in the coze-server container with no isolation at all \u2014 RCE for anyone who can author a workflow, in the container holding the DB credentials. Safe by default, but one env var from catastrophic.",
  "Code node is Python only; the sandbox runner returns `\"js not supported yet\"` for JavaScript.",
  "In chatflow mode, streaming is a per-node `StreamingOutput` toggle on the Output/Exit node; with it off the node uses `Invoke` and the whole answer arrives in one event, and nothing in the API reveals that the node is configured that way."
 ],
